"Projection mode first-class" in Alt-E means the wire-spec guarantees `Project` works on any conformant promise stack so assertion-type specs can say "this assertion sits anywhere; receivers must use Project to find it." Destination: DI-005-1.1 -> TODO-rivuk Decision Intent Log; after all four DFs land, `harness-spec-draft.md §1.1` gets one edit citing DI-005-1.1 through -1.4; DR-006 flips to decided. - [x] 021.161 Turn 161 (2026-05-03 02:57 UTC) -- "what's the difference between promise-stack and grid-pcid-payload?" -- (raises OQ-36.6 in concept; recorded one turn later). **Walked 2026-05-04.** Steve's question is a redundancy probe: if both promise-stack and grid-pcid-payload are envelope hypotheses, what is the actual difference between them, and are they really competing or is one a degenerate case of the other? The bot's answer laid out both hypotheses in detail. **Promise-stack:** message is `[]Promise` -- a CBOR array of nested promise frames; each `Promise` is a struct with fields `promiser` (AgentID/TransportID/RuntimeID/KernelID), `assertion` (open set including "this payload conforms to the spec at pCID P", "I authored this payload", "I forwarded this byte sequence unmodified", "I will perform action X if redeemed", etc.), `body` (bytes / promise-stack / nil), `evidence` (signature / MAC / "byte-arrival on authenticated channel" / nil), `ttl` (optional). The pCID is *one assertion type among many* -- it appears as a value inside one promise frame's `assertion` field. Receiver consumes top-down with `Wrap` / `Peel` / `Project`. Property: rich, recursive; the envelope is itself made of promises. **Grid-pcid-payload:** message is `grid <pcid> <payload>` -- a textual carrier prefix `grid`, followed by the protocol CID, followed by an opaque payload. Receiver dispatches on the pCID: "this is protocol P; hand the payload to P's handler." The payload could contain promises/signatures/structured fields, but the outer envelope is just three things. Property: flat, dispatch-only; the pCID is the *primary* element of the envelope. **Bot's nine-axis comparison table:** envelope cardinality (stack of N frames vs. exactly one frame); pCID's role (assertion-field value vs. envelope key); sender identity (promiser field per frame vs. inside payload or transport metadata); signature placement (evidence field vs. inside payload if protocol demands); forwarding chain-of-custody (each forwarder pushes a new outer promise vs. not modeled at envelope level); self-describing receiver behavior (top-down trust per frame vs. dispatch-only); library API (Wrap/Peel/Project vs. parse-grid-extract-pCID-handoff); encoding (CBOR array of maps vs. textual prefix + bytes per TE-hogus left open); what-gets-composed (promises recursively vs. inside payload by protocol rules). **Bot's key concession (the seed of OQ-36.6):** "Neither is a strict superset of the other. A grid-pcid-payload message can carry a promise-stack inside its payload (if the pCID names the promise-stack protocol). In that arrangement, promise-stack is one hypothesis about what fills the `<payload>` slot. A promise-stack message cannot easily carry a grid-pcid-payload structure as a peer at envelope level -- the grid carrier would have to be one specific assertion type encoded inside one promise frame, which is awkward." This asymmetry is the substance of Steve's turn-162 suspicion: if grid-pcid-payload can host promise-stack but not vice versa, then grid-pcid-payload is the more general envelope and promise-stack is a *specimen* that fills the payload slot of one specific pCID. The bot framed it as "competing hypotheses about where the layering happens: at envelope level (promise-stack) or inside payload (grid-pcid-payload)" but did not draw the asymmetry-implies-subordination conclusion that Steve would draw in turn 162. **Bot's closing offer:** "Want me to keep going on DF-36.5, or do you want to revisit DF-36.2/DF-36.3 in light of this distinction (e.g., reconsider whether the two are really separate hypotheses, or whether one is a degenerate case of the other)?" -- this is the bot recognizing the redundancy in its own answer but punting the choice to Steve. **Repo state:** no change in this turn; the OQ is in conversation only. Concretely, Steve sketches no specific layout, but the natural readings are `transports/git/group-session/draft--wire-lab-devs/` (substrate-above-protocol) or `transports/git-file-transport/group-session/...` (using the original TE-numan working name `git-file-transport` from the abandoned turn-164 plan). **Bot's answer reasons through both for and against, then rejects a substrate layer for DF-37.1 purposes.** **Bot's framing of what's actually being asked:** the question is whether substrate (git as the medium that ships bytes between collaborators) is something the directory tree should encode, or whether it's an implementation detail of how this particular repo happens to be shipped around. Bot establishes that `transports/<protocol-slug>/` answers "what protocol governs the wire bytes?" while a hypothetical `transports/git/` would answer "how do bytes get shipped?" -- two orthogonal questions on the same tree axis. **Bot's three arguments for adding a `git/` layer:** (1) *Honesty.* The current group-session instance only works because messages are committed and pushed via git; the §9 binding (per-author-branch, fetch-merge-push cycle) is git-specific. A `git/` directory makes the substrate explicit. (2) *Future substrates.* If group-session ever runs over a non-git substrate (NFS, S3, IPFS), `transports/git/group-session/` vs. `transports/s3/group-session/` would keep them separate. (3) *The TODO list literally calls this work "git-file-transport".* The continuity-summary completed-items list names the artifact as `protocols/git-file-transport.d/`, implying git-as-substrate is a first-class concept. **Bot's five arguments against:** (1) *Nothing else is git-specific in the protocol.* Group-session §4-§7 is pure canonical-bytes-and-CIDs; only §9 is the git binding, and §9 is structured as one possible binding -- the spec is structured so other bindings can be added without touching §1-§7. (2) *Risk of over-stratification.* One protocol with one binding and one instance currently exists; adding two layers above the instance is three levels of nesting for a tree with a single leaf. YAGNI says add levels when a second leaf forces the question. (3) *The substrate question is already encoded in §9.* A reader who lands in the instance directory and wonders how files get shipped follows the spec to §9 and gets the answer; the directory path doesn't need to repeat what the spec says. (4) *"git-file-transport" was a working name, not a final structural commitment.* TE-numan named the deliverable `protocols/git-file-transport.d/` because at that moment the work thought it was inventing a new protocol. This is the strongest argument against "binding" -- it's not just inappropriate vocabulary, it actively misleads. **Bot's candidate enumeration:** transport (taken; ambiguous); **feed** (Usenet's word; "strong fit"; "git feed," "rsync feed," "uucp feed"; site-declares-feeds pattern); link (FidoNet/BITNET; very generic; weaker); mailer (Sendmail; email-specific, confusing); relay (Sendmail's other word; implies forwarding hop, fits store-and-forward); carrier (already taken by `grid <pcid>` carrier line); substrate (descriptive but academic, not from any lineage); medium (generic); conduit (generic). That framing fails because: git is not transport-layer in OSI sense (it's an app-layer-and-above stack with its own substrate, TCP, under it); uucp similarly is a whole app-and-above stack; udp *is* OSI transport-layer, but rsync and unison are not. The substrates span multiple OSI layers, so "group-session binds to layer N below it" doesn't typecheck. **Bot's correct framing:** group-session messages are *bytes that need to be moved from author A to reader B*; the substrate is *whatever moves those bytes*; the substrate is not a layer below group-session, it's a *fully formed delivery mechanism* that group-session messages are *embedded in or transferred by*. The right historical analogy is **encapsulation and tunneling**, not layering. Email-over-UUCP is RFC 822 messages encapsulated as UUCP rmail invocations. Usenet-over-NNTP is articles encapsulated as NNTP IHAVE/SENDME exchanges. The encapsulation can change without changing what the article *is*. "The encapsulation is *horizontal* across multiple alternative mediums, not *vertical* down a single stack." **Concern (c) -- inverted layout.** Bot accepts "seems inverted" instinct. The yesterday-proposed layout (`bindings/git.md` *inside* the message directory) inverts the containment relationship: substrate (git) is the *container* of the messages, not contained by them. The git repo holds and ships the message files; message files don't hold the git repo. Putting `bindings/git.md` inside the message directory makes git look like a peer of the message files when actually git is *outside* and *around* the messages. If the same instance is mirrored over both git and rsync, *two separate substrates* each carry the *same* message set; messages are invariant, substrates are external observers/carriers. **Bot's three options for where substrate description should live:** **Option α** -- Substrate description outside the instance, alongside substrate tooling: `transports/group-session/draft--wire-lab-devs/` holds only `<cid>.txt` files plus a README; a separate top-level `feeds/git/draft--wire-lab-devs.md` describes how this instance is fed over git. Each substrate gets its own subdirectory at the top of the tree because configuration lives with the substrate's tooling. **Option β** -- Substrate description as a sibling of the instance directory: `draft--wire-lab-devs/` (messages) and `draft--wire-lab-devs.feeds/` (parallel sibling with `git.md`, `rsync.md`). The `.feeds` suffix mirrors the `.d` convention from `protocols/<slug>.d/`. **Option γ** -- Single INSTANCE.md manifest at instance root, no separate subdirectory: `<cid>.txt` files plus `INSTANCE.md` listing feeds, membership, etc. Bot recommends **Option γ** as closest to real systems: Usenet `newsfeeds` is one file per site listing all feeds; Sendmail `sendmail.cf` is one file per site listing all relays. No nested `bindings/` directory in either. Option α reasonable if substrate descriptions grow large or have own tooling, but for one substrate (git) and small spec, Option γ is simpler and matches precedent. **Bot's three corrections to yesterday's framing summarized:** (1) drop "binding" -> use **feed** with explicit Usenet lineage; spec family is `protocols/<slug>-feed.d/`; per-instance term is just "feed." Usenet-over-PromiseGrid: a group protocol that defines its messages as encapsulated Usenet articles; called usenet-relay-group or similar. the protocol must stay agnostic across this spectrum because picking a point too early may bake in cryptocurrency-pathology dynamics that promise-economy is supposed to avoid; wire-lab's job is to *test* points along the spectrum, not to pick one; the "everyone is their own central bank" framing is a property-not-a-policy: each site issues its own promise tokens denominated in its own units, and value comes from peers' willingness to accept those tokens, with no central issuer or unified currency -- (DISCUSSED, NOT WRITTEN); (b) **worm** (file.go) -- Write-Once-Read-Many file with a class\n header (block/tree) prefixed before content; hash is computed over header+body to prevent preimage attacks (this is structurally similar to the foundational CID promise discussed in UT-176.h, but at the file format layer rather than the CID layer); (c) **Block** (blob.go) -- leaf bytes; address = block/<algo>/<hash>; (d) **Tree** (tree.go) -- a Merkle node whose body is a newline-separated list of canonical paths to children (blocks or other trees); supports Read, Seek, Tell, Size, Verify, traverse, AppendBlock; (e) **Stream** (stream.go) -- a symlink in stream/<label> pointing at a tree's relative path; AppendBlock rewrites the symlink atomically via renameio to a new root (this maps cleanly onto the pointer-files-instead-of-symlinks discussion from turn 177; pitbase uses symlinks today, the design plan in turn-177 calls for CBOR pointer files); (f) **Path** (path.go) -- parses raw/abs/rel/canonical/addr forms; nests by 3-hex subdirs (4096-fanout) -- Steve's design note in db.go explicitly explains why 3 chars beats git's 2 (git uses 2-hex giving 256-fanout which causes performance issues with millions of objects on some filesystems); (g) **rabin** (chunker.go) -- thin wrapper around restic/chunker for content-defined chunking; defaults 512 KiB min / 8 MiB max (note: 8 MiB max is much larger than turn-177's recommended FastCDC ~16 KiB average; the parameter mismatch is real and per UT-177.d means wire-lab and pitbase will produce different chunk boundaries unless aligned); (h) **Object interface** (pitbase.go:135) -- Read/Write/Seek/Tell/Close/Size/GetPath; ## Decision Intent Log

- ID: DI-021-20260507-204144
  Date: 2026-05-07 20:41:44
  Status: active
  Decision: DF-V.2 locked Alt C ("split Carry-cluster semantics"). The Carry
    cluster (cluster J in `protocols/wire-lab.d/docs/ut-verification-matrix-20260507.md`,
    55 UTs in `dropped-thread-disposition-20260506.md` § "Carry (procedural /
    AGENTS-rule / cadence notes) (55 UTs)") does not have a single uniform
    closure semantic. Two sub-classes exist and must be classified separately:
    (1) in-turn self-corrections and purely historical observations whose
    substantive content is already captured by the walk note itself —
    eligible for the **resolved/retired** state under the loose reading of
    Caveat 1 (the walk note is the durable record); and (2) durable
    cross-session process rules (procedural lessons, AGENTS-style cadence
    rules, collaborator-anonymity discipline, foreground-DONE confirmation
    discipline, etc.) — these remain **transferred**, owned by an explicit
    procedure home (typically `AGENTS-ppx.md` or the relevant per-protocol
    procedure file), and are NOT considered resolved merely because the
    walk note recorded them.
  Intent: Stop conflating "the walk note noted it" with "the rule has
    landed." The split prevents both (a) leaving every Carry UT permanently
    transferred and (b) the looser failure mode of treating every Carry
    note as resolved just because it was observed.
  Constraints:
    - This DI locks ONLY the classification semantics. It does not
      authorize closure mechanics (no UT checkbox flips, no edits to the
      disposition memo's bullets, no edits to TODO-lilar's append-only
      walk notes). The closure-mechanism DF (currently DF-V.3 in the
      matrix) remains open and is required before any UT bullet is
      flipped.
    - This DI does not authorize the actual transfer of any specific rule
      into `AGENTS-ppx.md`. Which durable rules transfer, in what wording,
      with what enforcement, is a separate DF that must lock the per-rule
      transfer list before any AGENTS-ppx.md edit is made.
    - The Carry-cluster recount (how many of the 55 fall into sub-class 1
      vs sub-class 2) is a verification pass, not part of this DI; it is
      out of scope for this lock and is gated behind DF-V.3 along with
      the rest of the closure mechanics.
    - The matrix's existing 25 resolved / 20 transferred / 10 unclear
      Carry counts (loose reading) are NOT retroactively altered by this
      DI; they remain as recorded by the read-only Alt-V.4 pass. Any
      recount happens in the future closure pass under DF-V.3.
    - Append-only protections: `dropped-thread-disposition-20260506.md`
      bullets, TODO-lilar walk notes, and any pre-existing append-only
      audit memos remain untouched by this DI.
  Affects:
    - `protocols/wire-lab.d/docs/ut-verification-matrix-20260507.md`
      (this DI is referenced from a new "DF-V.2 lock" subsection appended
      to that file under "Provenance and follow-on DFs").
    - Future closure pass for the 55 Carry UTs (gated on DF-V.3).
    - Future `AGENTS-ppx.md` rule transfers (gated on a separate
      transfer-list DF; not authorized here).

Cross-references: TE-famar, DR-006, harness-spec-draft.md §1.1, TODO-rivuk subtasks 005.1-005.7.

Option 3's spirit (harness-level vocabulary TE + per-something locking) probably survives but with "different envelopes" replaced by "one envelope, multiple transports." The actual structural decision -- where TE-famar lives, whether TODO-rivuk closes, what the role of TE-famar is in the corrected one-envelope-many-transports model -- has not been re-asked or answered.

As of 2026-05-04, TE-famar still sits at `docs/thought-experiments/TE-famar-...md` with no Refinements section noting the reclassification, no Status change, and no link to a successor TE.
- [ ] **UT-156.c** Turn 156: **The bot's "harness-spec is wire-envelope-agnostic" claim is wrong and must be retracted, not committed.** Bot stated in turn 156: "harness-spec is the simulation-and-evaluation framework -- it should be wire-envelope-agnostic." **Steve's correction (2026-05-04 walk):** the wire-lab harness is supposed to describe the behavior of wire-lab itself and its simulation engines (LLM, ns-3, bespoke, etc.), and wire-lab must be able to simulate and evaluate design choices *for all layers*, including the outer envelope/layer shape itself. "The harness only knows about the outer envelope shape" is an error, because even the outer envelope is not yet decided -- `grid([pcid, payload])` is only a hypothesis. The correct framing is the apparatus-vs-specimen distinction (anticipated TE-havib territory): the harness is the *apparatus* that hosts and compares *specimens*, where specimens include candidate outer-envelope shapes (Env-1..Env-5 and others), candidate inner organizational disciplines (nested-set, ordered-set, multiset, etc.), candidate transports, candidate signing schemes, and candidate handler vocabularies -- all the way down. The harness therefore is *not* envelope-agnostic in the bot's turn-156 sense; it is *envelope-comparing*: it must be able to instantiate multiple candidate envelope shapes simultaneously and evaluate them under scenarios. **Resolution path:** (1) do NOT write "harness-spec is wire-envelope-agnostic" into any committed file; (2) capture the apparatus-vs-specimen framing instead, probably in TE-havib or in harness-spec itself, with explicit language that all layers are specimens and the harness compares them; (3) when (or if) any layer's design is locked, transition that layer from "specimen under test" to "baseline / default" with the harness still capable of comparing the baseline against alternatives.
- [ ] **UT-PSTK-origin** Turn 2 (2026-04-28 14:02 UTC): **"Promise stack" is the bot's mistranslation of Steve's phrase "a layered set of promises".** Turn 1 used "[pCID, payload, signature] envelope"; no "stack." Turn 2 query used "a layered set of promises" twice, never said "stack." Turn 2 answer (line 239 of the session log) translated this into "the wire model is now an ordered stack of Promise frames" and simultaneously invented the `Wrap / Peel / Project` three-op `promstack` library (line 251, line 392), which are operations that only make sense on an ordered sequence. Three category errors compressed into one sentence: (a) "layered set" -> "ordered stack" added an *ordered* property and a *stack* data structure that Steve had not implied; (b) "set" is order-irrelevant so a stack is the wrong data structure; (c) "layered" naturally reads as recursion / containment (a promise contains a promise contains a promise -- "promises all the way down"), not as a flat sequence of frames. From line 239 onward, the term propagates unquestioned through TE-famar, TE-muvuv, TE-magup, harness-spec §1.1, TODO-rivuk, DR-006, the `promstack` library naming, and ~150 conversational turns. **Resolution path:** the audit-input ledger and any future envelope-shape TE need to (1) preserve Steve's original "layered set" / "layered promises" / "promises all the way down" framing as the source of truth; (2) treat "promise stack" as one specific candidate envelope shape (not as established vocabulary); (3) explicitly contrast it with the recursive-containment model `grid([pcid, payload])` where `payload` may itself be a `grid([pcid', payload'])` -- this is recursion, not stacking; (4) mark TE-famar's framing as "derived from a misreading; reopen the prior question of what the layered-promise model actually is" rather than "axiomatic." **Research completed 2026-05-04** at `docs/research/nested-vs-stacked-envelopes-20260504.md` (6,633 words, 13 systems surveyed). Punchline from the synthesis: recursive nesting with a mandatory content-addressed type-token at every layer aged dramatically better than ordered frame sequences across capability systems (CapTP, OCapN), content-addressed networks (IPFS/IPLD CIDv1), signed-message formats (COSE/PASETO redesigning around JOSE's failures), and federated protocols (Matrix PDU, DIDComm v2). Frame sequences age well only at the transport / handshake layer (Noise, QUIC, TLS), not at the application-message-envelope layer. The `grid([pcid, payload])` hypothesis is structurally isomorphic to IPLD CIDv1, COSE_Sign1, and Matrix PDU; the "promise stack" framing is a category error at the envelope layer.

- [ ] **UT-161.c** Turn 161: **Bot's turn-161 list of `assertion` types is a finer-grained taxonomy than the five named pCIDs from harness-spec section 10a.** Bot's open-set list: "this payload conforms to the spec at pCID P", "I authored this payload", "I forwarded this byte sequence unmodified", "I will perform action X if redeemed", and the explicit "... (open set)" tail. Each of these is an assertion type a promiser can attach to a frame; they are orthogonal to the discourse-level pCID names from section 10a (`endorse-v1`, `contest-v1`, `counter-propose-v1`, `hypothesis-result-v1`, `proposal-checklist-v0`). The conformance / authorship / forwarding / future-action distinction is at a different layer than the discourse vocabulary -- the former describes *what the promiser is asserting about the frame body*, the latter describes *what role the message plays in a discourse*. This may inform DF-36.4 (discourse vocabulary home) by clarifying that the PT-recast (`conditional-promise` + `assessment-with-polarity`) handles discourse roles but not assertion-type taxonomy. Resolution path: when DF-36.4 unparks, decide whether the assertion-type taxonomy belongs in promise-stack.d (as a property of frames in that envelope) or in a separate vocabulary-of-assertions document, and whether it overlaps with PT primitives or is orthogonal.

If Steve confirms all four, the twig converges to branch (a) (merge as-is). If Steve denies any, the affected DFs unlock and the twig moves toward branch (b) anyway. Resolution path: this UT is the entry point for TE-havib unpark work; subtask 021.te36-twig is its task-list pointer. - [ ] **UT-164.d** Turn 164 (genesis message): **The membership-by-posting convention -- "the membership list is whatever set of From: values appears in the transport's commit history before the spec is frozen" -- is a load-bearing semi-formal rule that lives ONLY in m000's prose, not in any spec document.** This rule satisfies the closed-and-fixed-at-creation property (group-session §8) for the bootstrap instance, but it's an instance-specific convention, not a reusable rule. If m000 is later revised (and its CID changes per the rename-at-freeze rewrite of UT-164.e), the rule's authoritative source disappears. Resolution path: before group-session freezes, lift the membership-by-posting convention into either (a) `group-session-draft.md` as a recommended membership-discovery pattern, (b) `transport-spec-draft.md` as a per-instance metadata convention, or (c) a separate "transport-instance-bootstrap-conventions" document. The convention should also clarify whether membership freezes at spec-freeze-time or at first-message-time -- the prose says "before the spec is frozen," implying spec-freeze-time, but that creates a race condition with new posters arriving after freeze. Cross-references: group-session §8 (closed-and-fixed-at-creation); UT-164.e (rename-at-freeze).
- [ ] **UT-164.e** Turn 164 (Option A): **The rename-at-freeze operation -- "directory will be renamed from `transports/draft--wire-lab-devs/` to `transports/<pcid>--wire-lab-devs/` in a single mechanical commit, and every message's carrier line rewritten" -- is an append-only-violating operation in a transport whose §7 says append-only.** Two distinct violations: (1) directory rename changes paths (filenames change), which a strict append-only reader would reject as a rewrite of history; (2) carrier-line rewrite changes the bytes of every message in the transport, which changes every message's CID and breaks the Parents: chain. The genesis message m000's prose acknowledges the rewrite ("At freeze time, every message's carrier line will be rewritten...in a single mechanical commit") but does not address the append-only conflict. Resolution path: when group-session approaches freeze, decide between (a) carve-out -- amend §7 to permit a one-time freeze-rewrite operation with explicit constraints (no other content changes; commit message marks it as a rename-rewrite); (b) redefinition -- freeze creates a NEW transport instance under the real pCID, with a single "this transport supersedes <draft-cid>" message linking back; the draft directory remains untouched as a historical artifact (more aligned with append-only at the cost of one extra transport-instance directory); (c) no rewrite -- accept that messages exchanged before freeze use the literal string `grid draft:group-session` forever, and the directory keeps the `draft--` prefix forever for that instance, with future instances using the real pCID. Bot recommendation in turn 165 was Option A with the rewrite; the rewrite question itself was not flagged. Cross-references: group-session §7 (append-only); UT-164.c (freeze-gate coverage).

The merge-commit message itself says "Merge transport bootstrap (draft--wire-lab-devs) into ppx/main" so the substantive intent is recorded in human-readable form, but the branch-name metadata is wrong (refers to a TE-numan that was never drafted). Per Steve's "merge your own branches to ppx/main, then delete your own branches" rule, the branch is now deleted -- so the stale name persists only in reflog and merge-commit metadata, not as an active ref. - [ ] **UT-171.a** Turn 171: **Bot's argument-against #1 ("Nothing else is git-specific in the protocol") rests on §9 being a self-contained binding appendix that can be swapped out without touching §1-§7, but §9 is currently *inside* the group-session spec text rather than its own document.** Structural placement matters for the argument. If §9 truly is a swappable binding, putting it inline in the transport-protocol spec is a layering violation -- the protocol document mixes substrate-agnostic content with substrate-specific content. The bot's framing in turn 171 treats §9 as logically separable while leaving it physically unseparated. Turn 172 will surface this directly: bot will conclude "§9 is not 'the git binding' -- it's one of many bindings, and it leaks substrate concerns into a protocol spec that is otherwise substrate-agnostic" and propose lifting §9 out of group-session into its own binding-protocol document. The two turns are in tension: turn 171 uses §9's logical-separability as an argument *against* a directory layer; turn 172 uses §9's physical-non-separability as an argument *for* a binding-protocol family. The arguments don't actually contradict (logical separation vs physical placement are different concerns) but the rhetorical posture flips. Resolution path: when the binding-protocols work lands, decide whether §9 is extracted into `protocols/git-binding.d/specs/git-binding-draft.md` or remains inline with a non-normative pointer to a separate binding doc. Cross-references: 021.172 (binding-protocol family reframe); UT-168.b (§9 grew to eight subsections while staying "non-normative" -- the non-normative label was already doing load-bearing work).
- [ ] **UT-171.b** Turn 171: **Bot's argument-against #5 sketches an alternative encoding -- substrate as instance-manifest field rather than directory level -- but does not propose where that manifest would live, what its schema would be, or how it would interact with the pCID rename rule.** The bot offered "a `bindings/` field in each instance's manifest" and "a per-instance manifest field" as the proper place to encode substrate without specifying any of: (a) which file holds the manifest (a new `manifest.toml`/`manifest.yaml`/`manifest.json` in each instance directory? a section of the bootstrap README? metadata in a sidecar file?); (b) the schema (single binding-name string? list of substrate descriptors? structured records with addressing fields?); (c) interaction with the pCID rename rule (does the manifest get rewritten on freeze? does it carry pCID references?); (d) whether the manifest itself is a wire artifact subject to the canonical-bytes rule or an out-of-band administrative file. This is an implicit DF that gets neither a number nor a recommendation. Without those specifics, the manifest-field argument is hand-wavy and cannot be evaluated against the directory-level alternative on equal footing. Resolution path: if/when binding-pluralism work lands, draft a DF for instance-manifest design covering (a)-(d); cross-reference DF-37.1's argument-against-#5 as the origin of the idea. - [ ] **UT-171.c** Turn 171: **Bot's caveat (b) "defer until a second binding actually exists" applies a YAGNI rule whose triggering condition turn 172 immediately satisfies -- so the YAGNI deferral lasts exactly three minutes.** The bot's argument-against #2 ("Risk of over-stratification... YAGNI says we add levels when a second leaf forces the question, not before") and caveat (b) ("defer until a second binding actually exists") both invoke YAGNI to justify postponing the substrate-encoding decision. Three minutes later in turn 172, Steve enumerates six substrates (rsync, unison, uucp, udp, svn, cvs) plus git, satisfying the "second binding exists" trigger condition rhetorically (no actual second binding is implemented, but the *existence question* is settled). This pattern -- YAGNI-deferral immediately followed by scope-expansion that satisfies the deferral's triggering condition -- has appeared at least twice now (UT-167 sequential numbering deferred-then-reversed in 168, and now UT-171.c substrate-deferred-then-reframed in 172). Lesson: when a design conversation is *actively iterating*, YAGNI heuristics calibrated for steady-state codebases mis-fire. The relevant question in design conversations is not "is the second instance here yet?" but "is the second-instance question already in the conversation?" If yes, treat it as present. Resolution path: heuristic update for the bot's design-conversation mode; YAGNI applies to implementation, not to framing decisions where the alternatives are already in scope. Cross-references: UT-167.a (m000-m001 sequential numbering, deferred against turn 168); UT-167.e (cadence drift); 021.172 (where the deferred substrate question reopens).
- [ ] **UT-171.d** Turn 171: **Bot's recommendation question at the end ("Want me to proceed with Alt-1.A as the move, or does the substrate question still feel unresolved?") is structurally a two-part question that foregrounds Alt-1.A while implicitly inviting reopening of the substrate question -- and Steve takes the reopening invitation in turn 172.** The two-part framing is interesting because it acknowledges that the bot's own recommendation depends on a framing assumption (substrate-as-implementation-detail) that Steve might not share. By making the framing question explicit alongside the action question, the bot creates a clean off-ramp from the recommendation if the framing is wrong. Steve takes the off-ramp. This contrasts with the simpler "do you want me to do X?" framing used in earlier DF closings. Lesson: when a recommendation rests on a framing assumption the user has not explicitly endorsed, surfacing the framing question alongside the action question is more honest than asking only the action question. The cadence pattern is worth recording for future use. Resolution path: this is positive observation, not a discrepancy; document the two-part question framing as a useful pattern when a recommendation is contingent on framing-stability. Cross-references: UT-170.a (DF-37.1 ends up superseded via the off-ramp Steve took); 021.172 (where the substrate question is reopened); cadence-meta thread (UT-166.a / UT-167.e / UT-168.e / UT-169.c on ASAP-vs-confirmation drift).
- [ ] **UT-172.a** Turn 172: **The substrate-pluralism reframe explicitly retracts turn 171's argument-against #4 about the "git-file-transport" working name -- the bot's two-turn position on the same TODO-list artifact has flipped without an external trigger.** Turn 171 argued: "'git-file-transport' was a working name, not a final structural commitment" and used this to dismiss the continuity-summary's naming as evidence of substrate-as-first-class. Turn 172 reverses: "The TODO item that named the deliverable 'git-file-transport' was closer to right than I gave it credit for in my last response -- it sensed that git-as-substrate is its own first-class thing. The mistake was *where* to put it: not as a transport protocol, but as a binding protocol." Both turns are arguing in good faith; the difference is what "git-file-transport" is being measured against. Turn 171 measured it against transport-protocol classification (where it failed); turn 172 measured it against binding-protocol classification (where it succeeds). The flip is reasonable but worth flagging because the bot's confidence in turn 171's argument was high ("this was a working name, not a final structural commitment" is a strong dismissal) and that confidence reverses three minutes later. This is reasoning-vs-reasoning drift across two consecutive turns -- a different mode of drift from UT-169.a's reasoning-vs-action drift, but the same underlying pattern: high-confidence framing in turn N is overturned in turn N+1 by a Steve prompt that introduces context the turn-N analysis did not consider. Lesson: when an argument rests on classifying an artifact within a known taxonomy ("this is just a working name"), check whether the taxonomy itself might be incomplete before declaring the artifact misclassified. Resolution path: observational; document the pattern for future framing-stability checks. Cross-references: 021.171 (turn 171 argument-against #4); UT-169.a (reasoning-vs-action drift, the prior precedent); UT-170.b (TE-numan is now retroactively re-meaningful -- if git-binding becomes a real protocol, the abandoned TE-numan might deserve revival as the binding-protocol founding document).

- [ ] **UT-172.c** Turn 172: **Layout A vs Layout B (two presentations of how `bindings/` attaches to instances) is offered as a sub-decision under DF-38.2 / DF-38.4 with a tentative recommendation toward Layout B ("equivalently and possibly cleaner") but the difference is not formalized as its own DF.** Layout A: `bindings/` directly inside instance, alongside `<cid>.txt` message files. Layout B: `messages/<cid>.txt` and `bindings/<binding-name>.md` as siblings, with no message files at the instance-directory top level. The two layouts have meaningfully different consequences: Layout A keeps the current message-file-at-top-level convention (touching every instance dir is a smaller change); Layout B normalizes both axes (messages and bindings each live under their own subdirectory, symmetric structure) at the cost of a `messages/` migration for every existing instance. The DF-38.4 framing names this choice but conflates it with the broader question of bindings-as-siblings. Resolution path: when TE-sihih lands, separate the messages-tree-vs-flat decision from the bindings-attachment decision into distinct DFs (DF-38.4a and DF-38.4b, perhaps), since they could in principle be answered independently (`bindings/` could attach as a subdirectory regardless of whether messages move into `messages/`). Cross-references: turn 168 commit `19e9b37` (where filename = CID was locked, with messages directly in instance dir); turn 174-176 (where the eventual structural answer lands).
- [ ] **UT-172.d** Turn 172: **The proposed TE-sihih family of three protocol kinds -- carrier, transport, binding -- is a structural claim about the protocol taxonomy that has not been ratified; a *fourth* family was not considered.** The bot's three-family claim is plausible and matches existing conversation history (carrier protocols are the `grid <pcid>` envelope; transport protocols are group-session/udp-binding/etc; binding protocols are the new layer). But the enumeration is not justified beyond "we already distinguish [carrier] and [transport]; what's missing is [binding]." A fourth family is conceivable: identity protocols (how participant IDs work), governance protocols (how groups admit/expel members, change rules), naming protocols (how slugs/pCIDs interconvert), discovery protocols (how a participant finds a group to join), or bootstrap protocols (how a binding initiates first contact). The current group-session spec absorbs identity (§8), governance (the conditional-promise / reciprocal-promise model), naming (the slug -> pCID rename rule), and bootstrap (the founder-creates-then-others-join sequence) into one document. Some of those may genuinely belong in their own protocol families. Resolution path: when TE-sihih drafts the binding-protocols claim, also justify why exactly three families exist; if a fourth family emerges it should be named and either deferred or extracted. Cross-references: §8 of group-session-draft.md (membership question, multiple times revisited); turn 175's correction "We're not really representing sites very well. We're not representing the decentralized CAS" -- which may be naming yet another missing axis (sites and CAS-locations).
- [ ] **UT-172.e** Turn 172: **The bot's "clean spec evolution" claim -- that §9 can be replaced with a single short clause once binding protocols exist -- assumes the binding-protocol family is in place before any spec edit lands; this is a circular bootstrap dependency that is not surfaced.** The proposed sequence: (1) draft TE-sihih with five DFs; (2) walk DFs and decide; (3) draft `protocols/git-binding.d/specs/git-binding-draft.md` (containing §9's content); (4) only then can group-session §9 be replaced with the short clause. Steps 1-3 must complete before step 4 is even meaningful. During the interim, the spec is in a transitional state: §9 still says git-binding-things, but the binding-protocol structure does not yet exist on disk. The transitional state is brief but real, and it could go wrong in interesting ways (e.g., a participant tries to enforce §9 against an rsync peer because the binding-protocol doc does not yet exist to override it). The bot's recommendation does not surface this transitional-state risk. Resolution path: when TE-sihih drafts the spec edits, sequence them so the binding-protocol family arrives first and the group-session §9 deletion lands last; note explicitly in TE-sihih that the in-flight spec must remain locally consistent at every commit boundary. Lesson: structural refactors that cross multiple specs need an ordering proof, not just a destination state. Cross-references: TE-vipir (transport-tree-and-naming) for how previous structural refactors were sequenced; turn 169's Message-ID removal as a precedent for cross-spec/cross-message rehash that landed in a single commit; §4.7 strict-reader rule (UT-169.b) as an example of a transitional-state contradiction that did slip through.
- [ ] **UT-173.a** Turn 173: **The wire-lab-as-content-addressed-Usenet framing is load-bearing for many downstream decisions but has not been ratified.** Bot characterized wire-lab group-session as "essentially a tiny content-addressed Usenet" and used Usenet's substrate-pluralism (UUCP and NNTP running simultaneously for decades, byte-identical articles across substrates, free interchange) as the canonical precedent. If accepted, this framing implies importing many Usenet conventions: control messages (cancel/newgroup/rmgroup), Path: header for loop detection, History file for duplicate suppression, Xref: for cross-posting, and so on. None of these have been considered in wire-lab's group-session spec, which currently only has the §4 envelope (From/Date/Parents/Body) plus DAG-shaped Parents. If wire-lab is genuinely Usenet-shaped, then a future TE will need to address each of these convention imports (or explicit non-imports). If wire-lab is *not* Usenet-shaped, then the precedent argument weakens and the substrate-pluralism case rests more heavily on email/CORBA/SOAP rather than the Usenet-specific case. Resolution path: observational; before relying on Usenet precedent for further architectural decisions, ratify or qualify the wire-lab-as-Usenet framing in a TE. Cross-references: 021.173 Pattern C (substrate-agnostic message identity); UT-167.b / UT-167.c (§8 membership question, which Usenet handled via newgroup control messages); turn 175's correction "We're not really representing sites very well" -- Usenet's site model may be the missing concept.
- [ ] **UT-173.b** Turn 173: **DF-38.5's "binding" recommendation rests on W3C/WSDL precedent and conflict-avoidance with existing wire-lab vocabulary; turn 174 rejects it on different grounds (Usenet-gateway-vocabulary precedent), and the rejection is not because either precedent set is wrong but because bot and Steve are weighting different precedent sets.** Bot's three-part justification: (a) "binding" is the W3C/SOAP/WSDL term, most widely understood; (b) "transport" would conflict with existing wire-lab "transport protocol" usage; (c) "carrier" already means something else in PromiseGrid (grid <pcid> carrier line). All three points are factually correct. Steve's counter in turn 174 is also factually correct: Usenet gateways did not call themselves "bindings." The disagreement is *which precedent set is most relevant for naming*. Bot's reasoning leans on W3C-style formal protocol specs; Steve's on store-and-forward operational vocabulary. Both are part of the precedent enumeration in turn 173 -- the disagreement was already latent in the enumeration but not surfaced. Lesson: when multiple precedent sets exist for the same concept, a naming recommendation should explicitly identify which set is being weighted. Resolution path: turn 174-175 will close DF-38.5 by rejecting "binding" and accepting "feed" / "substrate" -- the rejection of "binding" is structural (the disagreement is over precedent-weight) rather than a fault in bot's reasoning. Cross-references: 021.174 (Steve challenges "binding"); 021.175 (Steve endorses "feed" and "substrate"); UT-172.b (DF-38.5 answered by negation rather than selection).
- [ ] **UT-173.c** Turn 173: **Bot's closing question dropped its framing-component (single-part "want me to draft TE-sihih now?") rather than the two-part action+framing pattern of turns 171 and 172. This is a cadence shift, not a cadence drift -- bot correctly recognized that externally-grounded framing doesn't need internal-confirmation.** Three turns of two-part questions (170 implicit "which alternative?", 171 explicit "or does the substrate question still feel unresolved?", 172 explicit "or does the framing itself need more thinking first?") lasted as long as the framing was internally contested. Once turn 173 spent its entire response justifying the framing externally (six precedent systems, three recurring patterns, fifty years of operational evidence), bot reverted to single-part: just the action question. This is observationally correct cadence -- a two-part question when framing is contested, a single-part question when framing is settled. Document this as a *useful* pattern: the question structure encodes the bot's confidence in the framing. Resolution path: this is positive observation; codify the cadence rule (two-part when framing is internally contested, single-part when externally validated). Cross-references: UT-171.d (two-part question pattern introduced); UT-172 walk (two-part question pattern sustained); turn 174 onward (subsequent questions will revert to two-part when Steve reopens DF-38.5).
- [ ] **UT-173.d** Turn 173: **The precedent enumeration is impressive but one-sided -- bot did not search for *negative* precedent (a system that tried binding-protocol-as-first-class and abandoned it).** Six positive precedents (email, Usenet, FidoNet, CORBA, SOAP, modern) and one counterfactual (HTTP-as-monolithic-substrate accumulated retrofits) is structurally a yes-bias. A negative precedent might exist: a federated messaging system that tried explicit binding-protocol separation and found it unworkable. Without searching for that case, the structural-confidence claim is slightly weakened. Possibilities worth checking: BITNET store-and-forward (mentioned briefly but not analyzed); IRC's federation model (RFC 1459 / RFC 2810; chose monolithic TCP and is criticized for it -- this is actually another positive precedent for separation); XMPP (federated, separated, generally considered successful but with complex deployment); the various failed P2P systems of the early 2000s (Freenet, Kademlia variants -- did they separate substrate from protocol cleanly?). Resolution path: in TE-sihih's background section, add a brief negative-precedent search; if no convincing negative precedent exists, state that explicitly to acknowledge the search was performed. Lesson: structural-confidence claims should report the search for counter-evidence, not just the search for supporting evidence. Cross-references: TE-sihih background section (when drafted); 021.173 the six positive precedents enumerated.
- [ ] **UT-173.e** Turn 173: **The git-itself-as-precedent observation is striking and may have non-obvious implications -- if git's own object/transport separation is the precedent, then wire-lab is in some sense building "git semantics on top of git transport" which is a specific kind of recursive structure.** Bot called git "the most relevant precedent of all because it's literally the substrate we're using." Git separates: (a) object format = content-addressed objects (blobs, trees, commits, tags); (b) transports = git:// ssh:// https:// file:// dumb-HTTP smart-HTTP bundle-files. Wire-lab's group-session is also content-addressed objects (CID-named .txt files) carried over a substrate (currently git, future rsync/uucp/etc). When wire-lab runs over git, the substrate's own object format and the protocol's own object format are stacked: wire-lab CIDs (sha-256 over canonical bytes) are stored as git blobs (sha-1 over file contents) which are committed as git tree entries which are pushed via git's wire protocol. There are *two* content-address layers, and they coincidentally agree on "this file's bytes are this hash." If wire-lab moves to rsync, the git-blob layer disappears but the wire-lab-CID layer remains. If wire-lab moves to a non-content-addressed substrate (rsync, uucp, S3-without-versioning), then the wire-lab-CID layer is the *only* content-address mechanism and the substrate becomes content-blind. This stacking has implications: under git, wire-lab gets free deduplication and free integrity-checking from the substrate; under rsync, those services must be provided by the wire-lab layer. The bot's substrate-pluralism claim assumes the wire-lab layer is always self-sufficient ("already substrate-agnostic by construction") but the deployment-properties under different substrates differ in real ways. Resolution path: TE-sihih should explicitly call out the layer-stacking that occurs when wire-lab runs over content-addressed substrates (git) vs. non-content-addressed substrates (rsync), and note that the binding-protocol spec for each substrate must account for the cardinality of CAS layers. Cross-references: turn 175's correction "We're not really representing the decentralized CAS" -- this is precisely about the CAS-cardinality question; UT-172.d (fourth-protocol-family possibility -- a CAS-substrate axis distinct from binding-substrate may be the missing fourth family).
- [ ] **UT-174.a** Turn 174: **The historical-networks-20260503 research doc just committed in the turn-173 cluster (commit `6890592`) used "binding" as one of multiple candidate terms with explicit non-recommendation, so it remains accurate; but its citation block in TE-sihih will need to point to a doc whose vocabulary table now favors "feed."** The doc's title is "Historical networks: substrate precedent for federated message protocols" and its naming-question section explicitly tabulates each precedent set's vocabulary (W3C `binding`, CORBA/libp2p/gRPC `transport`, Usenet/FidoNet `feed`, generic `substrate`, PromiseGrid `carrier`-already-taken). The doc deliberately uses "substrate" throughout as the neutral working term and notes the contested naming explicitly. Turn 174's "feed" decision (and turn 175's joint endorsement of "feed" and "substrate") fits *inside* the doc's existing naming-question table without rewriting -- the doc is forward-compatible. No edit needed yet, but when TE-sihih lands and cites the doc, the citation should note that the wire-lab decision was "feed" (with "substrate" as descriptive synonym). Resolution path: observational; doc was correctly written to be naming-neutral. Cross-references: commit `6890592` (historical-networks doc); 021.175 (Steve endorses both "feed" and "substrate"); UT-172.b (DF-38.5 answered by negation rather than selection -- now fully realized).
- [ ] **UT-174.b** Turn 174: **`udp-binding` is now retroactively misnamed in wire-lab's own vocabulary -- a previously-merged spec name is in queue for rename, and the spec's frozen pCID under wire-lab manifest may complicate the rename.** Bot flagged this as a side-finding and recommended scheduling it as a follow-up TE rather than addressing in TE-sihih. The structural problem: `udp-binding` was named when "binding" meant "a delivery mechanism for some abstract protocol"; under the new vocabulary, that mechanism is called a "feed," so the slug should be `udp-feed`. But: `udp-binding-draft.md` is a draft (not frozen, no manifest entry per `/tmp/spec check`'s 1-frozen/1-manifest/2-drafts state, where the 1 manifest entry is harness-spec). Wait -- actually the udp-binding spec is already drafted at `protocols/udp-binding.d/specs/udp-binding-draft.md`; if it's still draft (not frozen), the slug rename is feasible without breaking pCID-renaming-rule invariants because pCID is only computed at freeze time. So the rename is a *cheap chore now* and a *guaranteed bigger chore* after udp-binding freezes. Resolution path: either include a draft-stage rename of `udp-binding` -> `udp-feed` in TE-sihih itself, or schedule a fast-follow TE before udp-binding freezes. Cross-references: turn 174 bot's side-note flagging this; `protocols/udp-binding.d/` (current location); freeze-stage manifest-entry mechanics in TE-vipir (transport-tree-and-naming).
- [ ] **UT-174.c** Turn 174: **Option γ's INSTANCE.md absorbs membership-and-feeds in one file, which collides with §8's still-open membership-management question -- INSTANCE.md may need to be a wire artifact, or governance manifest, or both, and that decision was not surfaced in turn 174.** Bot proposed INSTANCE.md as "one manifest: membership, feeds, bootstrap notes" (per bot's structural sketch). But: (a) membership in group-session has been a recurring open question (UT-167.b, UT-167.c -- §8's closed-and-fixed-at-creation language vs §9's extensible-by-posting reality); (b) feeds are substrate descriptions whose addressing data (git remotes, rsync hosts) is *operational*, not protocol-level; (c) bootstrap notes are user-facing prose. These three categories have different epistemic statuses: membership decisions are protocol-bearing facts (governance), feed descriptions are deployment-bearing facts (operational), bootstrap notes are explanatory (rhetorical). Putting them all in one file is convenient but conflates three different kinds of authority. The Usenet `newsfeeds` precedent only handles feeds; membership in Usenet was managed by separate mechanisms (control messages). So Option γ's "single manifest" claim is partially false against precedent. Resolution path: TE-sihih should decide explicitly whether INSTANCE.md is (a) a wire artifact under canonical-bytes rules; (b) an out-of-band governance/operational manifest; (c) split into multiple files by category. Cross-references: UT-167.b / UT-167.c (§8 membership question); UT-168.d ("infrastructure files NOT propagated by merge" rule in §9.5 needs to classify INSTANCE.md); turn 175's site/CAS correction may further constrain INSTANCE.md's scope.
- [ ] **UT-174.d** Turn 174: **The encapsulation-not-layering reframe is structurally important but introduces vocabulary ambiguity: encapsulation has its own connotations including OSI's PDU-encapsulation and tunneling-protocol encapsulation; bot's "horizontal encapsulation" coinage is non-standard and may confuse readers.** Bot framed substrate-pluralism as "encapsulation across multiple alternative mediums, not vertical down a single stack" and called this "horizontal encapsulation." The framing is conceptually correct -- email-over-UUCP, Usenet-over-NNTP, etc. -- but "encapsulation" already has at least three established uses: (i) OSI/IP layering encapsulation (PDU at layer N+1 wrapped as payload of PDU at layer N); (ii) tunneling encapsulation (entire protocol stack wrapped as payload of another protocol stack, usually for crossing administrative boundaries); (iii) wire-lab's intended use (one protocol's bytes carried by alternative substrates as peers). Uses (i) and (ii) are vertical/stacking; (iii) is horizontal/alternative. The same word covering all three may mislead exactly the way "binding" was misleading. Bot's "horizontal encapsulation" qualifier helps but is not standard terminology. Possible alternatives: "carriage" (the substrate carries the messages), "transport" (taken), "feeding" (verb form of "feed"), or sticking with "feed" for both noun (the substrate-mapping spec) and verb (the act of moving messages). Resolution path: TE-sihih's framing section should pick one of: (a) accept "encapsulation" with explicit "horizontal across alternatives" qualifier on every use; (b) avoid "encapsulation" and use "feed" / "feeding" consistently as the single concept-and-action word; (c) borrow Sendmail/Usenet's verbs ("to relay," "to feed," "to peer") and avoid the abstract noun entirely. Cross-references: 021.173 Pattern A/B/C precedent map; UT-174.e (Pattern B remap to INSTANCE.md feeds section).
- [ ] **UT-178.a** Turn 178 (2026-05-03 19:14 UTC): **Sparse-CAS is now a foundational design assumption but no DF treats it.** Steve's wording: "you need to assume that no site has all CAS objects -- think big. our goal is to be able to store all known data; any site has sparse content." This invalidates any simulation, protocol, or proof that implicitly assumes full replication. Bot's turn-177 layered model assumed each site had "its CAS replica" which is consistent with sparse-CAS but the implications are deeper: (a) feed protocols must support "i don't have it; ask my peer" multi-hop fetch; (b) garbage collection becomes a per-site policy decision because no site can hold everything; (c) referential integrity (a tree pointing at chunks the local site doesn't have) is the *normal* case, not the error case; (d) sites must advertise *what they have* not *what exists*. TE-sihih needs a DF that mandates sparse-CAS as the design baseline and another DF that requires the simulation harness to exercise sparse scenarios. Cross-references: 021.178 walk note; UT-176.h (foundational CID promise -- sparse-CAS makes the bytes-stored-locally vs canonical-bytes question more pointed because the bytes may not be local at all).
- [ ] **UT-178.b** Turn 178 (2026-05-03 19:14 UTC): **The 'decides' word is the load-bearing point of the entire layered model and bot's turn-177 description glossed it.** Steve flagged: "that part about 'Bob's CAS receives the advertisement, decides it wants the chunk' -- the 'decides' is doing a lot of work -- that's where consensus, promise/trust relationships enter." The decision to fetch (or not) is where promise-economy, capability tokens, geofencing, conditional release, trust scoring, and rate limiting all live. Turn 177's flow description treated 'decides' as a single-step black box. TE-sihih needs a DF (or sub-DFs) specifying what inputs feed the decision, what outputs it produces, and what protocol surface exposes it (e.g., "L7 groups and L5 feeds communicate decision policy through a shared decision interface; L6 CAS exposes promised-vs-best-effort fetch primitives"). This is the cleanest articulation so far of what the L5/L6/L7 boundary actually means. Cross-references: 021.178 walk note; UT-177.e (promise economy elevation requires per-layer promise vocabulary -- 'decides' is *the* moment where layer-specific promises get cashed).
- [ ] **UT-178.c** Turn 178 (2026-05-03 19:14 UTC): **Layered accounting question is unanswered: does promise accounting live at L7, or per-layer at L5/L6/L7?** Steve raised: "layer 5 promises need to be able to be economic -- i'm concerned about the fact that the accounting for promises and trust might best be done up at layer 7 though, with some sort of communication between 7, 6, and 5 for what chunks to pull, keep, or advertise -- is there precedent for this in existing protocols?" This directly challenges turn 177's per-layer-promise-economy model (DF-38.12). The alternative is L7-as-accounting-authority with L5/L6 receiving directives. Bot's terse turn-178 answer did not address this question. Precedent likely exists in BGP's policy/transit distinction (the data plane forwards; the control plane decides), in CDN architectures (origin authority vs edge cache decisions), in Tor's onion-routing (rendezvous service decides; relays carry blindly), and arguably in IPFS bitswap (which is purely L5 carry-blindly with policy elsewhere). TE-sihih must choose: per-layer promise economies (turn 177's model), L7-centralized accounting with L5/L6 plumbing (turn 178's alternative), or a hybrid with explicit cross-layer signaling. This is now an open structural decision, not a settled one. Cross-references: 021.178 walk note; UT-177.e (promise economy elevation); DF-38.12.
- [ ] **UT-178.d** Turn 178 (2026-05-03 19:14 UTC, future-application class): **PromiseGrid-replaces-BGP is a major application Steve flagged as future consideration.** "could a promisegrid app replace BGP, to get away from BGP's vulnerabilities?" BGP's vulnerabilities are well-documented (route hijacking via prefix forgery, route leaks via misconfigured policies, slow convergence on link failures, no source authentication). A promise-economy-based inter-domain routing protocol could in principle bind route announcements to peer-relative trust scores, making hijacks costly to perpetrators rather than free. This is application-layer, not protocol-design-layer; it belongs in a future TE or roadmap item, not in TE-sihih. But it should be tracked because it informs L7's expressivity requirements -- if PromiseGrid must someday support BGP-class apps, L7 group protocols must be expressive enough to encode routing-policy semantics. Cross-references: 021.178 walk note; future TE candidate (capture later).
- [ ] **UT-178.e** Turn 178 (2026-05-03 19:14 UTC): **TE-sihih opening section is now mandated to be the capture narrative, not background.** Steve's "yes" to bot's turn-177 capture paragraph ("Email had no promise economy -> spam -> ...; Web had no promise economy -> SEO -> ...; Social had no promise economy -> engagement-driven harms -> ...") followed by "This needs to be in TE-sihih's background, possibly as the opening section. Without this framing, the layered architecture looks like just another protocol stack; with it, it's a coherent response to a specific set of failures." -- Steve confirmed both the content *and* its placement as the opener. TE-sihih cannot bury the capture narrative as background; it must lead with it. This shapes the entire document: what follows the capture narrative must be the response ("a layer with a built-in promise economy is not capturable"), not the technical spec first. Cross-references: 021.178 walk note; TE-sihih background section 1.
- [ ] **UT-178.f** Turn 178 (2026-05-03 19:14 UTC): **Anonymity-is-pathology is now a stated design stance with implications for group identity.** Steve's wording: "i believe (without proof) that anonymity on the internet is part of why the world is such a mess right now" combined with "all members of a group know each other in some way -- trust relationships exist." This is a design stance (group membership entails some level of mutual knowledge; pseudonymity may be allowed but pure anonymity is suspect by default). The implications for L7 group protocols: (a) groups must support membership rosters; (b) joining a group is a trust event, not a free action; (c) message provenance must be authenticatable to a member identity; (d) the protocol may *allow* but not *encourage* anonymous participation. This contrasts with platforms like Tor, Signal-with-pseudonyms, or anonymous Usenet -- PromiseGrid is taking an explicit stance against pure anonymity for federation-correctness reasons. TE-sihih must reflect this stance, even if just to acknowledge it as an open trade-off. Cross-references: 021.178 walk note; future DF candidate on group-membership semantics.
- [ ] **UT-178.g** Turn 178 (2026-05-03 19:14 UTC): **Dogfooding pressure now constrains TE-sihih's deliverable.** Steve's wording: "the devs and agents on our team need to be dogfooding this ASAP today, red-teaming and improving wire-lab by using wirelab-based messaging to communicate." This time-pressures TE-sihih: a paper spec is not enough; TE-sihih must drive toward a working pgmsg-class tool fast enough that the team can use it as their messaging protocol within (implicit) days, not weeks. This biases TE-sihih toward incremental, MVP-first scoping. The smaller-TE-sihih framing bot adopted in turn 179 (and refined further in turn 184) is partly a response to this pressure. Cross-references: 021.178 walk note; UT-177.b (DF count growth concern -- dogfooding pressure motivates *reducing* DF count, which contradicts the discovery-incomplete signal of UT-177.b).
- [ ] **UT-178.h** Turn 178 (2026-05-03 19:14 UTC): **Promisebase surfacing is the largest single context expansion of the session and changes TE-sihih's nature.** Steve's wording: "earlier, related, incomplete work by me and others is in promisebase: github.com/stevegt/promisebase" pivots TE-sihih from "design wire-lab's architecture from scratch" to "survey promisebase, find what's adoptable, design only what isn't." This is a structural reframe. The cascade through turns 179-184 (bot pivots to wholesale adoption, Steve catches it was based on design docs not code, bot reads code, finds db/ is real and tested while higher layers are vapor, fixes a regression, reframes TE-sihih as "adopt promisebase db/ as L6 substrate") would not have happened without this surfacing. TE-sihih is no longer about inventing wire-lab's L6; it's about adopting promisebase's existing L6 and defining wire-lab's distinctive L5+L7 contributions. The walk-note retroactive insight: turn 177's 15-DF model was overscoped because it assumed wire-lab would invent L6 from scratch; turn 178's promisebase surfacing collapsed roughly half of those DFs into "adopt from promisebase." Cross-references: 021.178 walk note; UT-177.b (DF count growth -- promisebase adoption *reduces* DF scope rather than expanding it); 021.181-184 walk notes (when written) for the cascade.
- [ ] **UT-178.i** Turn 178 (2026-05-03 19:14 UTC): **Interop constraint with libp2p/IPFS/ATPROTO restricts CIDv1/multihash/multibase choices.** Steve's wording: "we want to remain able to use (but not require) libp2p or IPFS where it makes sense to interact with those communities. likewise ATPROTO." This is a non-divergence constraint: wire-lab cannot pick CIDv1 codec values, multihash variants, or multibase encodings that would prevent bridge-building with libp2p/IPFS/ATPROTO. Practical implications: (a) use multicodec table values that exist in the public registry, not invented codecs; (b) use sha256 (multihash 0x12) at minimum because it's the IPFS default; (c) use base32 (multibase 'b') for canonical CID string form because it's IPFS canonical; (d) ATPROTO uses dag-cbor (codec 0x71) for records and base32 CIDs -- wire-lab dag-cbor adoption (planned per UT-177.c) is consistent with this. TE-sihih needs a DF that locks the multibase/multihash/multicodec choices to the IPFS-canonical defaults to preserve interop. Cross-references: 021.178 walk note; UT-177.c (CBOR adoption); UT-177.h (CIDv1 codec field for object-type distinction).
- [ ] **UT-178.j** Turn 178 (2026-05-03 19:14 UTC): **Multi-repo question is unanswered and structurally significant.** Steve's wording: "i'm starting to wonder if the actual message content should be in wire-lab, or if we should be using one or more other repos for that, particularly when it comes to testing large files. maybe wire-lab related messages go in wire-lab, others in other repos? can you even handle multiple repos at the same time in the same session? maybe later each simulated site is its own repo? game it out." This raises a major architectural question: is wire-lab a single repo containing both protocol-framework and message-corpus, or a multi-repo orchestrator where each simulated site is its own repo and wire-lab provides the harness? The latter is closer to real-world federation (each site is a separate trust domain with its own git history) and more honest to the sparse-CAS assumption (each repo holds only its site's chunks). Bot's terse turn-178 answer did not address this; it remains open. The implications for the current wire-lab repo are large: if each site is a separate repo, the current sites/<id>/ proposal (turn 177) is a placeholder for what should be cross-repo references. TE-sihih must decide. Cross-references: 021.178 walk note; UT-178.a (sparse-CAS); turn-177 sites layout proposal.

- [ ] **UT-pre149.dt4** Turns 41-42 (2026-04-29 15:50-15:52 UTC): **The "unfired conditionals are unassessed" trust-ledger rule is dropped.** Steve: "promises are assertions of state in the past, present, or future, often conditional." Bot derived a substantive ledger rule: "An unfired conditional promise whose condition never fires is unassessed -- it is neither kept nor broken; it does not move the trust ledger in either direction." The basic conditional-promise framing is in harness-spec sec.2 / TE-nibar / TE-rujak; the specific unfired/unassessed/no-ledger-movement rule is not in any committed file. grep for "unfired", "conditional.*ledger", "unassessed" returns no matches across committed corpus. The harness-spec sec.2 TrustLedger struct has fields `kept`, `broken`, `open_promises`, `score` but no field or prose covering the unassessed/unfired case. **Architectural weight**: rule affects how `open_promises` is treated when a condition never fires, how trust score computation handles the case, and how break-witnesses interact with unfired conditionals. **Resolution path**: file as a DI candidate against harness-spec sec.2.2 or as an OQ in TE-nibar/TE-rujak (via Cat-3 Refinements per the locked TE-dabol policy). Rule statement: "A conditional promise whose triggering condition never fires is unassessed: the promiser's kept and broken counts do not change; the promise remains in open_promises until the condition's evaluation deadline passes, at which point it is removed without ledger movement." Needs a DF before locking (deadline-evaluation question is the open variable). Cross-references: pre149-audit-report-20260505.md DT-4 (full detail); harness-spec sec.2.2; TE-nibar; TE-rujak.

- [ ] **UT-190.a** Turn 190 (2026-05-04 15:24 UTC, procedural): **Bot reported remote-branch enumeration as a narrated finding ("only main exists on the remote") rather than as raw command output, and the narrated finding was wrong -- the kv branch on stevegt/promisebase exists at the remote with 7 commits ahead of main and was missed entirely.** Walk-time ground truth (`git ls-remote --heads https://github.com/stevegt/promisebase.git`) returns two branches: main (d98b5d3) and kv (a4c20fa). This makes the answer ground-truthable by the user without re-running the command, and removes a class of narration-error possibilities (mis-summarizing, dropping a line, mis-reading the count). - [ ] **UT-190.c** Turn 190 (2026-05-04 15:24 UTC, procedural): **Steve's question used the plural "branches" -- "Can you examine the other promisebase branches?" -- which itself implied an expectation that multiple branches exist; bot answered "only main exists" without registering or flagging the expectation-mismatch.** The right behavior when a user's question presupposes a state that bot's findings contradict is to *flag the mismatch explicitly* rather than silently overriding the user's frame. Format proposal: "You asked about 'other branches' (plural) but I see only `main` on the remote (`git ls-remote --heads origin` output: <paste>). Did you have a different remote in mind, or did you mean historical branches that are now gone? I checked the latter too -- fskv/angela/jessica/ryan/stevegt all merged-then-deleted in 2021." The mismatch-flagging gives the user the chance to correct bot's understanding of the question scope -- in this case Steve might have replied "check `git ls-remote` again" or "there should be a kv branch", which would have caught the enumeration error sooner. **Procedural lesson**: bot's grammar-parsing of user prompts must include presupposition-detection. Plural nouns ("branches", "files", "commits", "people") presuppose multiplicity; specific names presuppose existence; tense-marked verbs presuppose temporal context. When bot's findings contradict a presupposition, bot must flag the contradiction explicitly. Cross-references: 021.190 walk note; UT-190.a (procedural cause); UT-190.b (substantive consequence); standing rule "make a recommendation" pattern (which presumes bot has correctly understood the question's frame).
