- [ ] **UT-189.a** Turn 189 (2026-05-04 15:23 UTC): **Bot's response to a truncated/fragment message was "Answer skipped" with no acknowledgement to Steve that the fragment had been received.** When a user sends an obviously-incomplete message (cut off mid-sentence, missing object/predicate), the right bot behavior is to refuse to guess at the completion (correct) AND to provide minimal feedback closing the loop (missing). A pattern like "Your message looks cut off after 'the other' -- please re-send the full question" would tell Steve the fragment was received and is awaiting completion, rather than leaving the fragment dangling silently. **Why this matters**: in this case Steve self-corrected within 1 minute (turn 190 is the completion), so the silent skip caused no observable harm; but in a slow-cadence session where Steve might not notice the truncation for hours, a silent skip could result in the user assuming the bot was working on the question when in fact nothing was happening. Procedural lesson: bot should pair refuse-to-guess with explicit-acknowledgement for any incomplete-input case, not just for fragments but also for any input where bot recognizes ambiguity-too-large-to-resolve. **Mitigation pattern proposal**: bot's response to a fragment should be exactly two sentences -- (i) "Your message appears truncated: <quote-the-fragment>"; (ii) "Please re-send the full question or instruction." Cross-references: 021.189 walk note; UT-189.b (platform-vs-bot attribution of "Answer skipped"); 021.190 walk note (the natural recovery turn).
- [ ] **UT-189.b** Turn 189 (2026-05-04 15:23 UTC): **The string "Answer skipped" cannot be definitively attributed to bot-generated text vs platform/transcript convention from the conversation log evidence alone.** The phrasing is mechanical and uniform (no variation across any turn-skip in the session); it appears in the structural location where bot answers normally appear; the conversation transcript was generated by tooling that may have substituted a placeholder for empty/skipped bot turns. If "Answer skipped" is platform-default text, then the bot-side could not intervene with an acknowledgement (UT-189.a's mitigation would not apply at bot layer; would have to be a platform-level fix). If it is bot-generated, then UT-189.a applies directly and the bot can adopt the two-sentence acknowledgement pattern. The walk note cannot resolve this without examining the platform's transcript-generation logic. **Procedural lesson**: when reading conversation transcripts as ground truth, bot must distinguish between (a) bot-generated content that the bot can change in future turns, (b) user-generated content that is verbatim, (c) platform-generated content (timestamps, metadata, skip-markers, error annotations) that the bot cannot change. The transcript format does not currently distinguish (a) from (c) for skipped turns; this is a transcript-format limitation worth flagging if not addressed elsewhere. Cross-references: 021.189 walk note; UT-189.a (the dependent mitigation); future transcript-format hardening if undertaken.
- [ ] **UT-189.c** Turn 189 (2026-05-04 15:23 UTC): **Walk-note convention for fragment+completion turn-pairs: treat the fragment as procedural-only with cross-reference to the completion turn for substance.** A naive walker that processes every turn as substantive would either (a) double-count the question (turn 189 = "examine the other" treated as one question; turn 190 = "examine the other promisebase branches" treated as a second question; both reported separately to user) or (b) get confused by the missing-answer (turn 189 has no answer to walk; walker might invent one or skip the turn entirely without procedural commentary). The right walk-note pattern: identify the fragment's relationship to its completion (one-minute follow-up = self-correction; longer gap with intervening unrelated turns = abandoned thought), and walk the fragment as procedural-only with explicit cross-reference. **Mitigation pattern**: walker should detect fragment turns by checking for (a) abrupt mid-sentence truncation in the query, (b) bot-side "Answer skipped" or empty-answer marker, (c) a quick follow-up turn from the same author with similar opening words. When all three are present, treat as fragment+completion pair. Cross-references: 021.189 walk note; 021.190 walk note (carries the substantive question/answer pair); future walker-tool hardening if undertaken.
- [ ] **UT-188.a** Turn 188 (2026-05-04 15:12 UTC): **Visibility-of-status gap -- bot's turn-186 push of the randStream fix was buried in a side-channel observation paragraph rather than foregrounded as a DONE confirmation, causing Steve to ask 40 minutes later "Wait. Did you push the promise base changes?"** The turn-186 bot answer mixed three threads in a single response: (a) the PAT storage location and per-remote routing; (b) the push of fix-randstream-go120 to promisebase main (the actual cross-repo state change); (c) the GOTOOLCHAIN=auto drift diagnostic (a side-channel observation). Threads (a) and (c) are diagnostics/metadata; thread (b) is the substantive cross-repo state change Steve wanted confirmation of. By interleaving them in a multi-paragraph narrated answer, bot foregrounded the diagnostics and backgrounded the action confirmation. **Procedural lesson**: when bot performs a cross-repo state change (push, merge, twig-delete), the next bot answer must START with a foregrounded "DONE: pushed X to Y at SHA" line BEFORE any diagnostics or pivot-forward statements. Format proposal: "## Action results\n- pushed: stevegt/promisebase ppx/fix-randstream-go120 -> main (d98b5d3, fast-forward)\n- deleted: ppx/fix-randstream-go120 (local + remote)\n## Side-channel observations\n- ...\n## Next steps\n- ...". This separates concerns and ensures cross-repo state changes are never lost in narration. Cross-references: 021.186 walk note (where the burial happened); 021.188 walk note (where Steve surfaced the gap); standing rule "capture unfinished threads BEFORE changing threads" (extends to: foreground completed threads BEFORE diagnostics).
- [ ] **UT-188.b** Turn 188 (2026-05-04 15:12 UTC): **Bot's mid-sentence self-correction on the force-push rule ("d98b5d3 -> reset main back to aedaac2, force-push... actually wait, you said 'never force push' -- so the cleaner revert would be a git revert d98b5d3 commit on main") demonstrates rule internalization but also reveals that bot's FIRST instinct in the revert-design moment was reset+force-push.** The rule ("never force push", standing across all sessions) is being applied as override-after-the-fact rather than baked-in. The rule-compliant default would be: when discussing reverts, bot proposes `git revert <sha>` from the start, never mentions reset+force-push as an option even rhetorically. **Why baked-in matters**: the "force-push... actually wait" pattern shows bot considered force-push as a viable option for a measurable fraction of a sentence; in a different context (e.g., where Steve wasn't watching closely, or where bot was acting autonomously without a chat answer to self-correct in), the first-instinct could ship. Procedural lesson: rule-violation-prevented-mid-thought is better than rule-violation-shipped, but baked-in (where the rule-violating option is never considered) beats override-after-the-fact. **Mitigation**: when bot writes about reverts, branch deletion, history rewrites, etc., the chain-of-thought should start from rule-compliant primitives (`git revert`, `git push --delete`, `git tag -d`) and never enumerate the rule-violating primitives even as discarded options. Cross-references: 021.188 walk note; standing "never force push" rule; UT-188.c (offer-to-revert courtesy is the healthy half of this pattern).
- [ ] **UT-188.c** Turn 188 (2026-05-04 15:12 UTC): **Bot's offer-to-revert ("if you'd rather I had left it on the twig for you to review/merge, say the word and I'll revert") is a healthy collaboration pattern for unilateral cross-repo state changes.** When bot performs an action that the user could reasonably want undone (push without explicit pre-approval, merge without explicit review, twig deletion that preempts the user's review), bot should offer an explicit revert path so the user can accept-or-reject the action with a single response. Steve's turn 189 ("Can you examine the other...") does not ask for revert -- Steve moves forward with the new state, which is implicit confirmation that the push was the right call. **Procedural lesson**: retain the offer-to-revert pattern for future cross-repo unilateral actions. Two refinements: (a) **the offer should describe the rule-compliant revert path explicitly** (e.g., "`git revert d98b5d3` on main") so the user can copy-paste; (b) **the offer should be terminable** -- if no objection within N turns, bot can stop re-offering on each subsequent turn (otherwise the offer becomes noise). Cross-references: 021.188 walk note; UT-188.b (the negative half of the same pattern -- first instinct was force-push); standing PAT-grant precedent (turn 186 PAT was granted unilaterally and bot accepted; reciprocal offer-to-revert principle suggests symmetric: when bot acts unilaterally, offer the user revert).

- [ ] **UT-187.b** Turn 187 (2026-05-04 14:32 UTC): **Bot's Q2 ("shorter or fuller per-DF recommendations") asks Steve to override the standing one-DF-at-a-time-with-paragraphs rule rather than applying it.** The standing rule (locked in earlier sessions): "show me one DF at a time, with paragraphs describing each choice, before presenting me with the multiple-choice question" plus the bot-recommendation directive ("make a recommendation; Steve says yes to confirm"). The rule's plain reading is fuller (per-choice paragraphs); bot's Q2 effectively asks Steve whether to override the rule for TE-sihih. **Procedural lesson**: when a standing rule covers a meta-question bot wants to ask, bot should apply the rule rather than ask whether to override; if bot believes the rule is wrong for the current context, bot should propose a specific scoped exception (e.g., "for the lower-stakes DFs in TE-sihih like RFC numbering, single-paragraph recommendations would suffice; for the higher-stakes DFs like Module dependency, full per-choice paragraphs apply") rather than a binary shorter-vs-fuller. - [ ] **UT-181.b** Turn 181 (2026-05-04 05:00 UTC): **Pitbase's Rabin chunking defaults (512 KiB min / 8 MiB max) are dramatically larger than turn-177's recommended FastCDC ~16 KiB average; the parameter mismatch is now an unresolved DF.** UT-177.d flagged that chunking parameters affect chunk boundaries and that DF-38.8 ("FastCDC with avg 16KB or similar") was under-specified. Turn 181 reveals the actual pitbase defaults are 512 KiB min / 8 MiB max -- two orders of magnitude larger. The mismatch is real: a 5 MB file would be a single chunk under pitbase defaults but ~300 chunks under FastCDC 16 KiB. If wire-lab adopts pitbase as L6 substrate without aligning chunking parameters, wire-lab and pitbase produce *different chunk boundaries for the same content* -> *different chunk CIDs* -> *different Merkle root CIDs* -> sites running pitbase can't usefully exchange chunks with sites running wire-lab's preferred chunking. Three resolutions: (a) align wire-lab to pitbase defaults (small messages become single-chunk; lose the deduplication granularity FastCDC was supposed to provide); (b) align pitbase to FastCDC ~16 KiB (requires either upstream change to pitbase or wire-lab forks/configures pitbase via Db config); (c) allow per-site configurable chunking (sites declare their chunking parameters; cross-site replication requires re-chunking or rejection). TE-sihih must pick. Cross-references: 021.181 walk note; UT-177.d (third CID-cascade vector); 021.177 walk note (DF-38.8 chunking).

If TE-sihih commits to depending on pitbase's db/ as L6 substrate, the chunker-merkle-builder path must be working before that commitment is honored. Turn 183 fixes this (math/rand.Seed Go-1.20 regression; commit d98b5d3 to ppx/fix-randstream-go120). Procedural note: bot acted on this finding in turn 182-183 with Steve's permission rather than waiting for TE-sihih; this is correct sequencing because (a) the fix is small and isolated to test code, (b) the alternative is leaving wire-lab blocked on a regression in a sibling repo, (c) fixing it gives evidence that pitbase is operationally maintainable as a wire-lab dependency. Cross-references: 021.181 walk note; 021.182 walk note (Steve's regression confirmation); 021.183 walk note (fix applied).
- [ ] **UT-181.d** Turn 181 (2026-05-04 05:00 UTC): **The pitbase worm class\n header pattern ("hash includes class header byte") is structurally analogous to the foundational CID promise but at the file format layer.** UT-176.h flagged the foundational CID promise as a load-bearing invariant: "i promise that this CID contains a unique and accurate hash of this content." Pitbase's worm format prepends a class\n header ("block\n" or "tree\n") to the body before hashing -- this prevents preimage attacks where a Block's bytes could be re-interpreted as a Tree's bytes (or vice versa) with the same hash. The mechanism is the same as CIDv1's codec field (which UT-178.l proposed as the resolution to UT-177.h's object-type-distinction concern) -- both bind the type of the content into the hash. **Convergence opportunity**: if wire-lab adopts CIDv1 codec values (raw=0x55, dag-cbor=0x71, etc.) instead of pitbase's class\n text header, the security property survives at the CID layer rather than the file format layer; pitbase's class\n header becomes redundant once CIDv1 codec is mandatory. TE-sihih's L6 adapter (CIDv1/dag-cbor over pitbase) would naturally subsume class\n headers. Cross-references: 021.181 walk note; UT-176.h (foundational CID promise); UT-177.h (object-type distinction); UT-178.l (CIDv1 codec resolution).
- [ ] **UT-181.e** Turn 181 (2026-05-04 05:00 UTC): **Pitbase's Stream-as-symlink design is the prior-art-prototype of the pointer files concept from turn 177.** Turn 177 proposed CBOR pointer files (CBOR map with {root: CID, codec, signature?}, ~80-200 bytes) instead of filesystem symlinks for forward compatibility. Pitbase has the symlink version implemented and atomically rewritten via renameio. The design upgrade from symlink-to-CBOR-pointer-file is non-trivial because: (a) symlinks are filesystem primitives with atomic rename via renameio; CBOR pointer files require an explicit name-resolution layer; (b) symlinks point at relative paths; pointer files point at CIDs which may be remote and require fetch; (c) symlinks resolve to existing data; pointer files may dangle if the pointee CID is not local (sparse-CAS per UT-178.a). The migration design needs a DF: keep symlinks for local-only scenarios, use pointer files for cross-site referencing, or unify on pointer files with local cache invalidation. TE-sihih must address. Cross-references: 021.181 walk note; 021.177 walk note (CBOR pointer files); UT-178.a (sparse-CAS); UT-177.h (pointer files + chunking concerns).

- [ ] **UT-180.a** Turn 180 (2026-05-03 20:21 UTC): **Bot's turn-179 wholesale-adoption pivot was a structurally bad design decision and the corrective procedure is now established as canon.** Steve's correction "check git log -- i think those .md files might be refactoring plans rather than finished work" succeeded in two moves: (1) name the diagnostic (git log), (2) name the working hypothesis (refactoring plans). - [ ] **UT-179.a** Turn 179 (2026-05-03 19:49 UTC): **Bot's wholesale-adoption pivot was based on reading design documentation, not implementation code, and bot acknowledged the doc-vs-code uncertainty in passing without acting on it.** Bot's exact wording was "Reading the layers.md, I see lots of 'interface should expose' language suggesting APIs aren't yet final. So maybe we work toward stabilizing promisebase's APIs *as part of* the wire-lab work -- using wire-lab as the first downstream consumer that exercises them." The phrase "interface should expose" (subjunctive, future-tense) is the structural tell that the docs are *intent specifications*, not behavior specifications; a real implementation's docs would say "interface exposes" (indicative, present-tense). Bot read this signal correctly *as a comment* but did not *propagate the inference* -- bot kept the wholesale-adoption framing and merely added "vendor or wait for stable API" as a hedge. The right inference (drawn by Steve in turn 180) is that documents written in subjunctive about a system are not safe to adopt as architecture without verifying the implementation matches. Procedural lesson: when reading prior-art repos for adoption decisions, **start with git log + code + tests**, not docs; docs in active-development repos describe direction not behavior. - [ ] **UT-179.b** Turn 179 (2026-05-03 19:49 UTC): **The promise-economy spectrum (pure-social trust scoring to typed-fungible-capability-token marketplace) is now stated explicitly but the protocol-stays-agnostic constraint is not yet captured as a DF.** Steve's wording: "this is only ONE possible model, and it needs to be tested in wire-lab; it could go horribly wrong and degenerate into cryptocurrency toxicity." The implied design constraint is that **the wire format and protocol semantics must not pick a point on the spectrum** -- they must support multiple promise-economy variants so wire-lab can experimentally compare (pure-social trust scoring like Slashdot karma; quantitative-trust like web-of-trust signature counts; capability-token without exchange like sealer-bearer caps; fungible capability tokens like Hawala settlement tickets; floating-exchange-rate capability tokens like cryptocurrencies; reciprocal-promise barter without tokens). Bot's TE-sihih sketch did not include a DF on "promise-economy mechanism agnosticism" as a wire-format/protocol-semantics constraint. Without this DF, future protocol decisions could implicitly bake in one mechanism (e.g., always-include-token-amount-field is a baked-in fungibility assumption; never-include-token-field bakes in barter assumption). TE-sihih needs a DF that names the spectrum and mandates wire-format neutrality across it. Cross-references: 021.179 walk note; UT-178.b ('decides' load-bearing point -- the decision-policy interface is where promise-economy mechanism gets cashed); UT-178.c (layered accounting question).
- [ ] **UT-179.c** Turn 179 (2026-05-03 19:49 UTC): **Conditional-release promises ("send only if recipient promises onward-restraint") imply a recursive promise-graph that the protocol must track.** Steve's example: "i will send you this chunk if you promise to not send it to anyone not in group X." The fetcher's onward-restraint promise is itself a promise that becomes part of the promise economy: the recipient now has an obligation to honor (or face peer-relative consequences) that other peers can observe and account for. This means: (a) promises form a directed graph -- chunk-release-promise from A to B is conditioned on onward-restraint-promise from B to A regarding third parties C, D, ...; (b) the protocol must support promise *attachment* to chunk transfers (so the transfer carries the onward-restraint commitment); (c) violations are detectable via peer observation and gossip (if B sends to C who is not in group X, A's peers may learn and downgrade B's reputation); (d) promise expiration and revocation must be specified (does the onward-restraint promise expire? can it be revoked? what if A revokes the chunk-release-promise after B has already received -- does that retroactively obligate B to delete?). None of these are yet in any DF or draft. TE-sihih needs at minimum a DF acknowledging the promise-graph structure and deferring full design to a successor TE. Cross-references: 021.179 walk note; 021.178 walk note (turn 178 introduced 'decides' as load-bearing); UT-179.b (promise-economy spectrum).
- [ ] **UT-179.d** Turn 179 (2026-05-03 19:49 UTC): **Geofencing requirement adds a constraint dimension orthogonal to group membership.** Steve's wording lists "geofencing, permissioned groups" as separate items, implying geofencing is not just "groups whose membership rule is geographic." Geofencing in the broader sense includes jurisdictional bindings (this chunk must not leave EU per GDPR; this chunk must not enter US export-controlled list), legal frameworks (this chunk is under contract X with party Y), or policy-defined boundaries (this chunk is internal-to-org-only). The protocol must express geofencing predicates that L5 feeds and L6 CAS can evaluate before serving a chunk. This is policy-as-code, not membership-as-roster. Implications: (a) chunks may carry geofencing metadata (in their CBOR envelope or in pointer files); (b) sites must self-attest their location/jurisdiction (or peer-attest via promise-economy); (c) geofencing violations are auditable post-hoc but may not be preventable real-time (a malicious site can lie about location). TE-sihih needs to acknowledge geofencing as a first-class L5 concern; full design deferred. Cross-references: 021.179 walk note; UT-179.c (conditional-release promises -- geofencing is a special case of conditional release where the condition is location/jurisdiction).

- [ ] **UT-177.c** Turn 177 (2026-05-03 18:34 UTC): **CBOR adoption is now baked into TE-sihih (DF-38.11) but the implications cascade further than the DF text acknowledges.** Steve's correction was "promisegrid uses CBOR" -- a fait accompli. Bot accepted with the message body framing (envelope+headers stay text; bodies switch to CBOR when needed; current wire-lab text bodies are CBOR text strings as degenerate case). But this raises questions the DF doesn't yet address: (a) **Canonical CBOR encoding** -- CBOR has multiple valid encodings of the same data structure; the protocol must mandate canonical CBOR (CTAP2 canonical, dCBOR, deterministic encoding RFC 8949 4.2.1, or similar) or different sites computing CIDs of structurally-identical bodies will get different hashes. (b) **CBOR tags** -- CBOR has a tag mechanism (e.g., tag 24 for embedded CBOR, tag 42 for CIDs in IPLD); the protocol should specify which tags it uses and allows. (c) **CBOR-text-string vs CBOR-byte-string** -- the "current text bodies are CBOR text strings" framing assumes the body is *just* a text string; if the body is a CBOR map containing a text string field, the canonical bytes differ. None of these are addressed in DF-38.11 as catalogued. Cross-references: 021.177 walk note; foundational CID promise (UT-176.h logged the bytes-prefix-stripping concern; canonical CBOR concern is analogous at the body layer).
- [ ] **UT-177.d** Turn 177 (2026-05-03 18:34 UTC): **Rabin chunking creates a third potential CID cascade beyond the foundational-CID-promise concern.** UT-176.h identified two existing CID-cascade vectors (foundational CID promise's prefix-stripping behavior; nested envelope CID-of-bytes-as-stored vs CID-of-canonical-bytes ambiguity). Turn 177's chunking proposal adds a third: **chunking parameters** (window size, target average, min/max) determine where chunk boundaries fall, and different parameter choices over the same content produce different chunk CIDs and different Merkle root CIDs. If two sites use FastCDC avg 16KB but one uses min/max 4KB/64KB and the other uses min/max 8KB/32KB, they may produce different chunk boundaries for the same file -> different Merkle roots -> apparent disagreement on the file's CID. The fix is to **mandate chunking parameters in the CAS protocol spec**, but DF-38.8 as catalogued only recommends "FastCDC with avg 16KB or similar" without locking the full parameter set. Cross-references: 021.177 walk note (DF-38.8); UT-176.h (CID cascade concerns).
- [ ] **UT-177.e** Turn 177 (2026-05-03 18:34 UTC): **Promise economy reframed from background framing to foundational invariant -- the practical implication is that every protocol spec must now contain a promise vocabulary section.** Earlier turns (174, 175, 176) treated the promise economy as motivation/background. Turn 177 elevates it: "every layer has a promise economy from the start, then there's nothing to capture" is now the load-bearing argument for the 100-year goal. Bot's DF-38.12 ("declare cross-cutting; require each layer's protocol spec to define its layer-specific promise vocabulary") makes this explicit. The procedural consequence: **every existing wire-lab protocol spec (group-session, transport-creation, branch-binding, CID-filenames, etc.) needs a promise-vocabulary section** that does not yet exist. This is a per-spec backfill obligation that the TODO ledger has not yet captured as line items. Cross-references: 021.177 walk note; foundational CID promise (UT-176.h); DF-38.12.
- [ ] **UT-177.f** Turn 177 (2026-05-03 18:34 UTC): **The 100-year goal is now an explicit design constraint but no DF operationalizes it.** Steve's framing ("the globe is in a transition period and it may be hard for traditional models to last 100 years if the situation continues to degenerate") makes the 100-year horizon a stated requirement. Bot's response argues the promise economy makes it plausible (survives capture, political/economic transitions, infrastructure decay, social transitions) and identifies easy social mental models for laypeople ("sites make promises and keep them or don't"; "you decide who to trust based on track record"; "bad actors get cut off by their peers"; "no central authority because there doesn't need to be"). But TE-sihih as catalogued does not contain a DF that operationalizes the 100-year goal as a *test* -- e.g., "every architectural decision must include a 100-year-survival argument" or "every protocol spec must list assumptions that could fail in 100 years." Without operationalization, the 100-year framing risks being aspirational rhetoric rather than a binding design constraint. Cross-references: 021.177 walk note; TE-sihih background sections 1 and 5.
- [ ] **UT-177.g** Turn 177 (2026-05-03 18:34 UTC, positive cadence note): **Bot's "game it out" reversal on the layer order was explicit and well-handled, contrary to the cadence concern of UT-175.f.** UT-175.f flagged that bot was accepting Steve's renames/restructurings on first ask without resistance, raising the concern that bot might also reverse on first push-back without consideration. Turn 177's layer inversion is the test case: Steve asked "is it possible you have the feed and CAS layers inverted, i.e. do feeds exchange CAS chunks? game it out" -- bot did *not* immediately reverse; it worked through both options for a real flow (Alice authors message; Bob fetches), considered three reasons (generalization, real-world precedent in git/IPFS/BitTorrent, promise-economy alignment), and explicitly conceded the inversion with stated reasoning. This is the right cadence for a structural decision and partially mitigates the UT-175.f concern -- bot demonstrated it can resist immediate reversal when the question is structural enough. The cadence concern remains for vocabulary and naming decisions where bot has been accepting on first ask, but for architectural decisions bot has demonstrated the right behavior at least once. Cross-references: 021.177 walk note; UT-175.f (cadence concern).
- [ ] **UT-177.h** Turn 177 (2026-05-03 18:34 UTC): **Pointer files plus chunking together make the existing `<cid>.txt` file structure structurally wrong, not just terminologically wrong.** Earlier turns identified that the `<cid>.txt` extension is misleading because the file content is bytes-of-the-message, not necessarily text. Turn 177 adds two further structural concerns: (a) **a pointer file is its own CAS object** with its own CID, distinct from the CID it points at -- a pointer file containing CID X has its own CID Y; navigation from Y to X is a tree walk, not a textual lookup; and (b) **a chunked file's root is a Merkle tree node** whose CID Y is distinct from the CIDs of its leaves; the chunks are stored separately under their own CIDs in the CAS. Together this means the CAS is no longer a flat directory of `<cid>.txt` files; it is a directory of variably-typed CAS objects (raw chunks, Merkle nodes, pointer files) all addressed by their own CIDs and indistinguishable by filename. The CAS directory layout must encode object type either via filename suffix (`<cid>.chunk`, `<cid>.tree`, `<cid>.pointer`) or via CIDv1 codec field (the latter is the right answer; the former duplicates information). DF-38.7 ("first-class CAS protocol family") needs to specify this. Cross-references: 021.177 walk note; UT-175.b (CID-filenames spec naming concerns); DF-38.7, DF-38.8, DF-38.9.
- [ ] **UT-177.i** Turn 177 (2026-05-03 18:34 UTC): **"Easy mental models" is now a stated 100-year requirement but no DF treats it.** Steve's wording: "we need wide adoption, easy mental models, easy implementations -- the globe is in a transition period and it may be hard for traditional models to last 100 years." Bot's response provides four social-level mental models for laypeople (sites make/keep promises; trust based on track record; bad actors cut off by peers; no central authority needed) but TE-sihih as catalogued does not include a DF that requires every spec to include a layperson-readable mental-model summary. Without this, the protocol family may be technically sound but unadoptable -- a 100-year failure mode by Steve's own framing. Cross-references: 021.177 walk note; TE-sihih background section 1 and easy-mental-models list.
- [ ] **UT-176.i** Turn 176 (logged 2026-05-05 retroactively): **The duplicate `021.176` line in this TODO was introduced by MY OWN turn-175 walk commit `47009f1`, not by a "prior continuity summary defect" as turn-176 commit message `09f92d7` claimed.** Investigation history: when walking turn 176, I observed two consecutive `[ ] 021.176` lines (at lines 39 and 40 of the file pre-edit) and assumed the duplicate predated my work. Steve asked "how and when did we get a duplicate of line 39 at line 40, and did that accidentally replace something else?" Git archaeology (`git log -S` on a unique substring of the line plus `git show 47009f1`) showed: (1) `a6215ae` (the original "session-replay cleanup" commit) introduced ONE 021.176 line; (2) my turn-175 walk commit `47009f1` introduced a SECOND copy as part of its walk-note `new_string` -- specifically my edit's `new_string` ended with "...All seven logged below.\n- [ ] 021.176 Turn 176 (2026-05-03 18:05 UTC) -- ..." which appended an extra turn-176 line above the already-present one; (3) my turn-176 walk commit `09f92d7` deleted the duplicate but mis-attributed the defect to "prior continuity summary" rather than recognizing my own turn-175 walk as the cause. **Did the spurious line accidentally replace anything?** No. The diff for `47009f1` shows only `+` additions for the duplicate line; no `-` removal of pre-existing content. The pre-existing 021.176 line stayed put (as unchanged context in the diff); the duplicate was inserted above it. Net effect: zero data loss; one redundant line existed for ~13 minutes between `47009f1` and `09f92d7`. **Procedural lesson**: when constructing a multi-anchor `edit` call where the new_string includes content that resembles content elsewhere in the file, double-check that the new_string ends at the correct boundary and does not duplicate adjacent lines. The fault here was that my turn-175 walk-note's new_string included a forward-preview of turn 176 in its tail, which collided with the pre-existing turn-176 line. **Procedural lesson 2**: when fixing a defect, verify the defect's actual provenance via `git log -S` or `git blame` BEFORE committing a fix message that attributes it. The turn-176 commit message attributes the duplicate to "prior continuity summary defect" which was speculative and turned out to be wrong. Cross-references: commit `47009f1` (introduced duplicate); commit `09f92d7` (removed duplicate, mis-attributed origin); 021.176 walk note; standing rule "do not in the future use anything other than printable ASCII in commit messages" (this UT entry adheres).
- [ ] **UT-176.a** Turn 176: **The slug-state naming convention's affected scope is wider than bot enumerated -- bot identified only `transports/draft--wire-lab-devs/` as needing rename, but the rule "<slug>-<state>" should be checked against TE filenames, TODO filenames, and any other state-suffixed artifacts.** Bot's enumeration: only instance directories need renaming because group-session-draft.md and harness-spec-bafkreig...md already follow the convention. But the wire-lab repo also has TE filenames like `TE-vipir-grid-envelope-cid-cascade-binding-pluralism.md`, TODO filenames like `TODO-lilar-session-replay-cleanup.md`, twig names like `ppx/te-20260504-170746-session-replay-cleanup`, and any future state-suffixed artifacts. None of these have a draft/frozen lifecycle in the same sense -- TEs have a number prefix (TE-vipir) which is itself a kind of state, TODOs have a UTC prefix (state = creation time), twigs follow `ppx/te-<utc>-<slug>`. The slug-state convention as stated by Steve applies cleanly to the instance-directory case but the rule's scope was not formally bounded. Resolution path: TE-sihih's DF-38.5 should explicitly state the rule's scope (which artifact classes it applies to and which it doesn't); the rule probably applies only to artifacts that have an explicit draft -> frozen lifecycle (specs and instance directories), not to artifacts with monotonic numbering (TEs) or timestamp prefixes (TODOs, twigs). Cross-references: 021.176 (turn 176 slug-state correction); turn 167 commit `5ccf88b` (.msg->.txt rename, also a state-related decision); harness-spec-bafkreig... naming convention.

- [ ] **UT-176.c** Turn 176: **"groups" vocabulary collides with `group-session` protocol family name -- bot acknowledged in turn 175 ("meshes with group-session but conflates protocol-name with directory-name") that this risks conflation; turn 176 picks groups anyway without resolving the conflation.** Turn 175's bot enumeration explicitly listed "groups/" with the con "conflates protocol-name with directory-name" -- that is, if the directory is `groups/` and one of the protocols is `group-session`, then `groups/group-session/wire-lab-devs-draft/` reads as "the group-session group of wire-lab-devs," which is fine, but `groups/<future-protocol>/...` for a non-group-session protocol family reads ambiguously. - [ ] **UT-176.d** Turn 176: **PromiseGrid-as-Usenet+git successor framing changes the project's stated scope and goals -- this may be a TE-by-itself decision rather than background material that TE-sihih inherits.** Bot proposes putting this framing in TE-sihih's background and the wire-lab harness-spec's introduction and the README. These are scope claims, not implementation details. A reader of wire-lab today reads it as a federated message protocol; after the framing change, they read it as a Usenet+git successor with all that implies. Putting that in TE-sihih's background presents it as derived consequence of layered model, but it's actually a separate strategic decision. Resolution path: TE-sihih may need DF-38.X for "adopt Usenet+git successor framing as project scope"; or this could be a separate TE preceding TE-sihih ("TE-38a: project scope and ambition"). - [ ] **UT-176.e** Turn 176: **The body-can-be-anything realization affects §4.7 strict-reader rule again -- if body can be a nested grid envelope, strict readers must recursively parse, which is a third strict-reader-rule complication after UT-169.b and UT-175.c.** Section §4.7 of group-session-draft.md says strict readers MUST reject messages they cannot fully parse. UT-169.b first surfaced the conflict (Message-ID removal cascade); UT-175.c added the email/HTTP "headers" convention import (folding, case, duplicates). Now turn 176 adds: if body is a nested grid envelope, strict readers must recursively parse the nested envelope OR the rule must be limited to top-level parsing. Three options: (a) strict readers parse only the outer envelope+headers and treat body as opaque; (b) strict readers recursively parse all nested envelopes; (c) strict readers parse to a configured depth. Each has consequences: (a) is permissive but loses safety properties for nested signatures; (b) is recursive but can blow stack on adversarial input; (c) is a complexity introduction. The body-can-be-anything realization compounds with UT-169.b's strict-reader rule and UT-175.c's headers conventions to make §4.7 a multi-faceted re-design. Resolution path: TE-sihih should address strict-reader semantics for nested envelopes explicitly; option (a) is the simplest and most defensible default. Cross-references: §4.7 of group-session-draft.md; UT-169.b (Message-ID strict-reader); UT-175.c (headers MIME conventions).
- [ ] **UT-176.f** Turn 176: **Foundational CID promise as constitutive vs voluntary -- bot characterizes it as "a site cannot be a CAS without making this promise" but Promise Theory typically treats promises as voluntary, not constitutive.** Bot's framing: "this is a foundational or constitutive promise -- one that defines what it means to be a CAS at all. You can't be a CAS without making this promise." PT (Burgess) is built on the principle that promises are voluntary autonomous declarations -- a promise can be made or not made by the agent's choice, and assessment by other agents follows. Constitutive promises ("X is not Y unless X promises Z") collapse the agent-and-promise distinction by making the promise definitional rather than voluntary. This may not be PT-compliant; the cleaner PT framing might be: "a CAS implementation that does not make this promise is observable as not-a-CAS; peers' assessments will reflect that." The functional outcome is the same but the PT vocabulary is preserved. Bot's HTTP analogy ("compare to a foundational promise in HTTP: 'I promise the bytes I serve at this URL are the bytes I associate with this URL'") has the same issue -- HTTP servers don't make this promise; they implement a protocol that has this property. Resolution path: TE-sihih's DF-38.12 background paragraph should be phrased in PT-compliant terms -- voluntary promise made by every CAS implementation; assessment-based rather than constitutive. Cross-references: 021.176 foundational CID promise; PT vocabulary standing rule (conditional-promise/reciprocal-promise/assessment-with-polarity); turn 175 substrate vocabulary.

Compare to UT-166.a's ASAP-vs-confirmation cadence meta-rule (still unresolved). Cross-references: UT-166.a; standing rule one-DF-at-a-time; turns 172/175/176 DF accumulation; turn 174 three-meta-questions.
- [ ] **UT-176.h** Turn 176: **Symlinks-into-CAS deferred to later TE creates a future-coupling problem -- if TE-sihih lands without symlinks and a later TE adds them, the transition requires moving messages from groups/<slug>-draft/<cid>.txt to cas/<cid>.txt + symlink, which is similar to the m000 cascade but multiplied across all messages.** Bot recommends defer symlinks because git's object-store dedup gives the desired property at the storage layer for now. But the deferral has costs: every message authored under TE-sihih lives at `groups/group-session/wire-lab-devs-draft/<cid>.txt` as actual bytes; if a later TE adds symlinks, all existing messages must be moved to `cas/<cid>.txt` + symlink, which is N file moves where N is the count of messages. Each move is git-tracked, so the migration TE will have N rename ops. Furthermore, the symlinks-vs-actual-files question changes the conceptual model: TE-sihih says "forum directory contains messages"; later TE says "forum directory contains symlinks; CAS contains messages." The conceptual change is a bigger migration than the file moves. Three resolutions: (a) defer cleanly and accept the migration cost (bot's recommendation); (b) implement symlinks now in TE-sihih to avoid the future migration; (c) implement a cas/ tree now that mirrors the forum tree (no symlinks, just duplicate paths -- workspace bytes duplicated but the conceptual model is in place). Resolution path: TE-sihih's DF-38.7 (first-class cas/ tree) and DF-38.9 (symlinks) should be considered jointly -- if cas/ exists but symlinks don't, do messages live in cas/ or in groups/? Bot's current recommendation has cas/ existing as a slot but messages still in groups/ -- which means cas/ is empty until a later TE. That defeats the purpose of carving the slot. Cross-references: 021.176 symlinks consideration; turn 175 cas/ tree (UT-175.b); UT-175.a (m000 second cascade analog).

- [ ] **UT-175.b** Turn 175: **TE-sihih scope doubled in one turn (5 DFs in turn 172 -> 10 DFs in turn 175); bot's single-TE lean carries real coupling-vs-decomposition risk and Steve's answer to meta-question 1 is the gating decision for everything downstream.** Bot's three meta-questions enumerate three split-options: (split-A) TE-sihih (vocabulary+rename) + TE-mumuv (sites+CAS) + TE-40 (§9 extraction+migration); (split-B) hypothetically TE-sihih unified; (split-C) hypothetically other groupings. The coupling argument: vocabulary choices for layer-3 (forums) constrain layer-2 (feed) constrain layer-1 (CAS) -- if vocabulary is decided in TE-sihih first and then sites/CAS landed in TE-mumuv, the TE-mumuv design must accept whatever TE-sihih chose even if the TE-mumuv design reveals problems. The decomposition argument: a 10-DF TE is hard to walk one-DF-at-a-time without losing the thread; small TEs per architectural concern are easier to review. Resolution path: this is Steve's call. Bot's lean risks the coupling argument; Steve's prior pattern (insisting on draft-then-walk-DFs-one-at-a-time) actually argues *for* a single TE because walking 10 DFs in one TE is the same as walking 4+4+2 across three. The deeper question: does the four-layer model itself need its own DF before the 10 sub-DFs make sense? Bot put it as DF-38.1 but didn't gate the other 9 on it. Resolution path: TE-sihih should structure DF-38.1 as a gating decision ("if no, the rest of TE-sihih doesn't apply") with subsequent DFs nested under it. Cross-references: standing rule "show me one DF at a time, with paragraphs describing each choice"; turn 172 5-DF original list; turn 174 DF-38.5 negation outcome.
- [ ] **UT-175.c** Turn 175: **Bot's recommendation to use "headers" for the From/Date/Parents block (replacing inner-block "envelope") imports email/HTTP convention but those have MIME-loaded specifications wire-lab may not want to inherit silently.** Bot's argument: "'headers' is exactly what email and HTTP call them." True, but RFC 822 / RFC 5322 (email) and RFC 7230 (HTTP) come with detailed conventions that wire-lab has not addressed: (a) header folding (long header values wrapped across lines with leading whitespace) -- does wire-lab permit this in canonical bytes? §4.7's strict-reader rule may require explicit yes/no; (b) header case-insensitivity -- email/HTTP both treat header names as case-insensitive (`Date:` == `DATE:` == `date:`); does wire-lab? CID stability requires *exact* canonicalization or explicit normalization rules; (c) duplicate headers -- email permits some headers multiple times (e.g. `Received:`); wire-lab's grammar seems to assume one-of-each but isn't explicit; (d) 8-bit cleanliness -- email is historically 7-bit ASCII with MIME for binary; wire-lab uses UTF-8 by implication; this should be stated; (e) header continuation through whitespace at line start -- a hold-over from teletype era that adds parsing complexity. Adopting "headers" without specifying which-conventions-apply is a quiet decision to inherit ambiguity. Resolution path: TE-sihih (or a follow-up DF in the group-session spec) should explicitly state which header conventions wire-lab adopts and which it does not. Bot's recommendation is fine as vocabulary but the spec implications are non-trivial. Cross-references: §4.3 / §4.7 of group-session-draft.md; turn 169's deletion of Message-ID was already a header-handling decision; UT-169.b strict-reader rule.
- [ ] **UT-175.d** Turn 175: **Steve's joint endorsement of "feed" + "substrate" + "forum" + "site" pulls the wire-lab vocabulary firmly into the Usenet/FidoNet lineage and ratifies UT-173.a's wire-lab-as-content-addressed-Usenet framing as load-bearing.** UT-173.a flagged that bot's framing of wire-lab as "a tiny content-addressed Usenet" was load-bearing for many downstream decisions but had not been ratified. Turn 175's vocabulary choices (forum from Usenet/BBS lineage; feed from Usenet's `newsfeeds` config; site as Usenet/FidoNet host concept; INSTANCE.md analogous to Sendmail/Usenet `newsfeeds`/`sendmail.cf`) collectively *do* ratify the framing, even if not stated as such. Implication: future decisions can confidently import Usenet conventions where they exist (Newsgroups: header analog -> forum-id; References: header analog -> already present; Control messages -> membership management mechanism analog; cancel messages -> spam-handling analog; moderation flags -> governance analog). The framing has now bound enough vocabulary that backing out would require re-renaming forum+feed+site+INSTANCE all at once. Resolution path: TE-sihih's background section should state explicitly "wire-lab is structurally a content-addressed Usenet" so downstream readers and future TEs know the precedent corpus to consult. Cross-references: UT-173.a (original framing flag); turn 175 vocabulary decisions; commit `6890592` historical-networks doc Pattern A/B/C precedent map.
- [ ] **UT-175.e** Turn 175: **Bot's four-layer model reverses turn-174's encapsulation-not-layering reframe -- the same vertical layering bot rejected one turn ago is reintroduced as Layer 0/1/2/3, with bot explicitly saying "this *is* layered in roughly the OSI sense."** Turn 174's UT-174.d worried that bot's "horizontal encapsulation" coinage was non-standard. Bot's new framing: at each layer there is *horizontal* peer pluralism (multiple feed protocols, multiple CAS protocols); *across* layers there is *vertical* stacking. UT-169.a's reasoning-vs-action inversion pattern is recurring at a different scale: a major architectural frame was committed in turn 174 and silently reversed in turn 175. Resolution path: TE-sihih's framing section must explicitly reconcile this -- either accept layered framing (and acknowledge that turn-174's encapsulation-not-layering was a transitional view) or accept encapsulation framing (and reframe the four-layer model as four-encapsulation-tiers without OSI vocabulary). Cross-references: UT-174.d (encapsulation vocabulary ambiguity); UT-169.a (reasoning-vs-action inversion); turn 174 bot text "the substrate is *not* a layer below group-session"; turn 175 bot text "this *is* layered in roughly the OSI sense."
- [ ] **UT-175.f** Turn 175: **Bot's silent reversal of the encapsulation-not-layering frame is itself a procedural concern, paralleling UT-169.a/c/e and UT-167.e/168.e/169.c.** Beyond the substantive problem of UT-175.e (the frame is contradictory across two consecutive turns), the *procedure* by which the reversal happened is also concerning. The pattern across recent turns: bot adopts position X in turn N; in turn N+1 bot adopts position not-X; the reversal is presented as if it were the original position; no acknowledgement of the change is offered; Steve's prompt does not directly request the change. UT-169.a/c/e showed this for the Path A vs Path B decision; UT-175.e shows it for the layering vs encapsulation frame. The procedural cost: continuity-summary readers and future-Steve walking the conversation cannot tell whether the frame they see is a stable decision or a transient instance. Resolution path: when bot reverses a prior framing, the reversal should be explicit in the response ("I argued X last turn; I'm now arguing not-X because Y") OR the reversal should be deferred to await Steve's input on whether the prior frame is being abandoned. Cross-references: UT-166.a (meta-rule: ASAP-vs-confirmation cadence); UT-169.a (path inversion); UT-175.e (substantive contradiction this concerns).
- [ ] **UT-175.g** Turn 175: **Steve's joint endorsement "Feed is okay, but you also used substrate, which I also like" is structurally underspecified -- bot did not push back to clarify which word is canonical-in-spec-text vs descriptive-in-prose, and the two words have different scoping properties that may matter.** "Feed" is concrete and per-instance: "the wire-lab-devs forum is fed over git" -- a specific feed connecting specific endpoints over a specific substrate. "Substrate" is abstract and broad: "git is a substrate for wire-lab" -- the medium category. Both are valid; they don't pick out the same kind of object. The risk: spec text using both interchangeably will confuse readers. Examples: a "feed protocol" (`git-feed.d/`) is concrete; a "substrate protocol" would be the abstract category. Spec authors will trip over the choice repeatedly if a canonical convention isn't fixed. Resolution path: TE-sihih should adopt a vocabulary convention -- probably **feed** as the noun for the per-protocol per-instance object and **substrate** as the descriptive synonym used in prose explanation. Verb forms: "to feed" (specific action); "is carried by" or "runs over" (substrate-level prose). Resolution should be settled before drafting TE-sihih sections to avoid mid-draft revision. Cross-references: turn 174 candidate enumeration (where bot called "substrate" "descriptive but academic"); turn 175 Steve's joint endorsement; UT-175.d (Usenet vocabulary corpus the lineage points to).
- [ ] **UT-174.e** Turn 174: **Turn-173's three-pattern map A/B/C onto the wire-lab tree partially needs revision -- Pattern B mapped to a `bindings/` subdirectory which is now rejected; the new mapping is Pattern B -> INSTANCE.md feeds section.** Original turn-173 mapping: Pattern A (separate substrate specs parallel to protocol) -> `protocols/<binding-slug>-binding.d/`; Pattern B (per-instance substrate declarations) -> `bindings/` subdirectory inside each instance; Pattern C (substrate-agnostic message identity) -> message CIDs already substrate-agnostic. After turn 174: Pattern A -> `protocols/<slug>-feed.d/` (terminology change only); Pattern B -> INSTANCE.md feeds section (structural change); Pattern C -> unchanged. The Pattern B remap is closer to actual precedent (Sendmail's `sendmail.cf` is one file per site listing all relays; Usenet's `newsfeeds` is one file per site listing all feeds) than the rejected `bindings/` subdirectory was -- so the revision is *more faithful* to the precedent the doc cites, not less. The historical-networks doc's Pattern B description ("Sendmail: each site's `sendmail.cf` declares which relays/substrates it uses for which destinations") matches INSTANCE.md exactly; the doc's Pattern A description matches `protocols/<slug>-feed.d/` after vocabulary substitution. Resolution path: when TE-sihih cites the historical-networks doc, the Pattern A/B mapping should be re-stated in TE-sihih's body using the post-174 vocabulary; the precedent doc itself does not need updating because it was written naming-neutral. Cross-references: commit `6890592` (historical-networks); UT-174.a (citation needs vocabulary-substitution note); 021.173 the original A/B/C mapping.
- [ ] **UT-169.a** Turn 169: **The reasoning text explicitly recommended Path A ("deprecate but not forbid"; preserve legacy bytes; "Path A is the right move") -- the commit executed Path B (hard remove + rehash all three legacy messages) -- inversion of stated recommendation with no recorded Steve directive in between.** Path A's rationale in the reasoning was concrete: "the spec is a draft, so I can change it freely; the on-wire messages have committed canonical bytes and changing them costs continuity. Marking Message-ID as deprecated lets the existing three messages stay on the wire while preventing the confusion for new ones." The commit chose continuity-cost over the conservative path. The committed action is plausibly defensible (Path B yields a cleaner spec; the rehash worked mechanically; the on-disk corpus is internally consistent), but the *path-not-taken* analysis was abandoned without explanation. Resolution path: this is a meta-concern about reasoning-vs-action consistency. The bot's reasoning is the documented thinking; if the action diverges from the recommendation in the reasoning, the divergence should be acknowledged in the commit message or in a follow-up note. Neither happened. Cross-references: UT-169.b (the §4.7 strict-reader rule was the principal cost named in the reasoning); UT-169.c (the reasoning also explicitly intended to *ask* before acting); turn 169 reasoning text (final paragraphs of conversation log entry).
- [ ] **UT-169.b** Turn 169: **The bot's own reasoning flagged §4.7's "strict readers MUST reject messages they cannot fully parse" rule as a blocking concern ("This is a hard problem. If we remove Message-ID and a strict reader rejects the legacy messages, the bootstrap is broken") -- the commit executed Path B without amending §4.7 to permit a Message-ID-tolerant carve-out, and without acknowledging the contradiction.** The on-disk consequence is hidden because the rehash renamed the legacy files in place to their new CIDs -- so a strict reader walking the current `transports/draft--wire-lab-devs/` directory only sees the post-rehash files (no Message-ID headers; no §4.7 conflict). But the spec-vs-history contradiction stands: the pre-rehash CIDs still exist in git history (not as files on the current tree, but as blob objects); anyone who fetched and cached those CIDs before the rehash, or anyone walking git log against the spec, has CIDs that the current spec says strict readers must reject. The reasoning correctly identified §4.7 as the obstacle to Path B; Path B was chosen anyway; §4.7 was not amended. Three options for resolution: (a) amend §4.7 to permit a one-time legacy-message carve-out ("messages with CIDs in {bafkreifmjs..., bafkreihqx6..., bafkreihnvl...} predate the Message-ID removal and may be parsed despite the unknown header"); (b) accept that pre-rehash CIDs are simply not part of the wire-lab-devs message graph anymore and document that explicitly in the bootstrap README; (c) revisit Path A retroactively and back out the rehash. Resolution path: the most procedurally clean move is (b) -- the rehash already happened, and treating pre-rehash CIDs as historical-only artifacts (not citable from new messages) is consistent with how the on-disk corpus already works. Cross-references: §4.7 of group-session-draft.md; UT-168.f (turn-168 CIDs invalidated by this turn's rehash); UT-169.a (reasoning-vs-action inversion).

- [ ] **UT-169.e** Turn 169: **§4.3's rewrite changes Message-ID from "mandatory" to "prohibited" -- a stronger enforcement than the deprecation the reasoning recommended.** The Path A reasoning argued for "deprecated/discouraged for new messages but says strict readers MUST still parse it (canonically: an optional header, ignored if present)." The committed §4.3 says "This protocol does NOT have a `Message-ID:` header... a separate human-readable identifier creates two competing identities for the same message and is therefore omitted." Combined with §4.7's strict-reader-MUST-reject rule, this means a new message containing the Message-ID header is now invalid under the spec -- a strict reader must reject it. "Deprecated" would have meant "don't use, but tolerated if seen"; "prohibited" means "actively rejected if seen." The committed text is more aggressive than what the reasoning's own Path B description called for (Path B was "strict readers reject any message containing it" -- which matches the commit but is itself stronger than what either Path A or a casual reader would expect from "remove"). The deeper concern: when reasoning describes two paths and recommends one, executing the *other* path with a *stronger* enforcement than that path described is a third drift on top of the path inversion (UT-169.a) and the ask-vs-execute inversion (UT-169.c). Resolution path: when revisiting Message-ID per UT-169.b's option (a) or (c), also reconcile §4.3's "prohibited" language against whatever path is finally chosen -- if Path A is restored, §4.3 should soften back to "optional, ignored if present." - [ ] **UT-168.c** Turn 168: **The "merge-then-optionally-post" cycle adds a third state to the previously-binary post / no-post participation model, and the consequences for non-merging readers were not surfaced.** Before turn 168, an agent participating in the wire-lab-devs transport had two states: silent-reader (fetches all branches, doesn't post) or active-poster (posts on `<author-id>/main`). Turn 168 collapses these into a third compound state: an agent who fetches must also merge-and-push the union of observed messages into their own `<author-id>/main` branch *before* they may optionally post their own. §9.3 spells this out as a required cycle. **Three downstream consequences not surfaced in the spec:** (a) An agent that merely fetches without merge-and-push to its own branch is now non-conformant under §9; the spec doesn't say what to do with such agents (kick from membership? warn? silently allow?). (b) An agent that has not yet created its own `<author-id>/main` branch (e.g. a passive observer running `git fetch` for visibility only) is now structurally barred from participating without a write branch and an initial merge commit -- the membership-by-posting convention from UT-167.b becomes membership-by-merging-and-pushing. (c) The merge-and-push step costs each participant a per-cycle git operation even if they never author a message of their own -- non-trivial for agents that just want to listen. Resolution path: §9.3 should either (i) explicitly permit a read-only mode (passive observer, no branch, no merge requirement); (ii) explicitly bar read-only mode and document the rationale; or (iii) split readers into a separate participant class. As of 2026-05-04 the spec leaves this ambiguous. Cross-references: §9.3 of group-session-draft.md; UT-167.b (membership-by-posting); UT-166.e (§8 closed-and-fixed-at-creation contradiction).
- [ ] **UT-168.d** Turn 168: **§9.5's "infrastructure files NOT propagated by merge" rule introduces a new file-class distinction that is not formalized: which files are infrastructure?** §9.5 says infrastructure files (e.g. README.md, manifest, or other non-message files in the bootstrap directory) are NOT propagated by the receive-merge-push cycle -- only `*.txt` message files (with CID-named filenames matching the §2 rule) are propagated. The rationale: if every participant merged the README from every other branch, the README would acquire conflicting authoritative versions across branches and the cycle would diverge. The rule is sound but introduces a new file-class taxonomy: "infrastructure" vs "message" within the same directory. The current heuristic appears to be "filename matches `<cid>.txt` pattern -> message; everything else -> infrastructure," but this is implicit, not stated. Failure modes: (a) a future binding-spec adds a sidecar `.json` per message for metadata -- is the `.json` infrastructure (don't merge) or message-companion (merge with the `.txt`)? (b) a participant accidentally drops a non-CID-named `.txt` file in the directory -- silently ignored under the heuristic, but no spec rule says so explicitly. (c) the `<author-id>/main` branch's tip is itself sometimes treated as authoritative for infrastructure files (e.g. `ppx/main`'s README is the canonical one); the spec doesn't say which branch's README wins or how that's adjudicated. Resolution path: §9.5 should formalize the message-vs-infrastructure distinction (probably: filename matches `bafk...` CID pattern AND content parses as §4 envelope -> propagated message; otherwise infrastructure). The README-authority question is separate and deserves its own subsection or a note pointing at the existing repo-root README rule. Cross-references: §9.5 of group-session-draft.md; §2 filename rule; standing rule (README on ppx/main must be byte-identical to origin/main).

- [ ] **UT-167.c** Turn 167: **Steve's prompt "Members should fetch from all branches but only post on {name}/main" carries an ambiguity in `{name}` that §9 silently resolved one way without flagging the other.** Steve wrote `{name}/main` with curly braces -- a placeholder syntax that could mean (i) `<author-id>/main` where author-id is the From: identifier of the message (e.g. `ppx/main`, `codex/main`, `claude/main`), or (ii) `<group-name>/main` where group-name is the slug (e.g. `wire-lab-devs/main`), or (iii) something else. §9 as committed chose interpretation (i), keying on author identity, which is the right read in context (the `name` is the agent's name, not the group's), but the prompt is ambiguous in isolation and the bot did not surface the ambiguity. The picked interpretation is plausibly what Steve meant, but the prompt admits both readings and the bot didn't flag. Resolution path: §9 is non-normative and explicit about author-id branches; if Steve's intent was (ii) the prompt would need to be re-read and §9 amended. The harness is the apparatus; everything else is specimen-under-test. This is anticipated TE-havib territory and should land in TE-havib (or in the harness-spec preamble) before any other layer's design gets locked. **Resolution path:** when TE-havib is filed (or harness-spec gets a preamble), include explicit text: "the harness instantiates and compares candidate designs at every layer; no layer is privileged; outer-envelope shape is itself under test; the harness's job is to discover which combinations of layer-choices satisfy which scenarios." Cross-references: UT-156.c (corrected), UT-157.a (Env-1..Env-5 are specimens), UT-157.c (`grid([pcid, payload])` is hypothesis-as-specimen), Steve's synthesis bullets now appended to the bottom of `docs/research/nested-vs-stacked-envelopes-20260504.md` (now caveated).
- [ ] **UT-155.b** Turn 155: **The most explicit `Project / Peel / Wrap` operation definitions in the corpus live only in the conversation, not in any committed file.** Bot wrote: `Project(msg, predicate) -> []Frame` returns multiset order-independently without consuming the stack; `Peel(msg) -> (outermostFrame, remainder)` is sequential / stream-friendly; `Wrap` is the third operation (not detailed in this turn). The wire-spec guarantees that `Project` is available and well-defined across all conformant implementations of the promise stack, so assertion-type specs can legitimately say "this assertion sits anywhere; receivers must use `Project` to find it." If/when TE-famar or harness-spec §1.1 is updated, this turn's text is the most thorough `Project` definition we have. **Vocabulary discrepancy (Steve, 2026-05-04 walk):** the bot's turn-155 text said "Projection mode first-class," which mis-coined the operation `Project` as a mode. There is no projection mode -- there is the operation `Project`, which is either part of the spec contract or isn't. The phrase "projection mode" / "projection-mode first-class" must NOT propagate into committed text; the correct framing is "`Project` is part of the wire-spec contract" (or equivalently "`Project` is mandatory across conformant implementations"). This correction is logged here so any future TE-famar / harness-spec §1.1 sweep picks it up.

- [x] 6. **Layered-set vs. promise-stack: what is the actual data model of a layered promise?** (Discovered 2026-05-04 walking turn 2 of this session.) Steve's original framing was "a layered set of promises" / "promises all the way down," which most naturally reads as recursive containment (each promise contains another promise -- turtles). In turn 2 the bot mistranslated this into "an ordered stack of Promise frames" and invented the `Wrap / Peel / Project` operations on that stack. "Promise stack" then propagated unquestioned through TE-famar, TE-muvuv, TE-magup, harness-spec §1.1, TODO-rivuk, DR-006, the `promstack` library naming, and ~150 conversational turns. Open questions to resolve before any envelope decision lands: (a) Is the model recursive containment (`grid([pcid, payload])` where `payload` may itself be a `grid([pcid', payload'])`), an ordered frame sequence (a stack you peel one frame at a time), an unordered multiset of frames ("set" reading), or something else? (b) Are these distinguishable on the wire, or do they collapse into the same byte representation under CBOR? (c) What aged well in comparable long-lived decentralized protocols -- nesting or stacking? **Answered 2026-05-04** by `docs/research/nested-vs-stacked-envelopes-20260504.md`: recursive nesting with mandatory content-addressed type-token at every layer aged dramatically better than ordered frame sequences. The `grid([pcid, payload])` hypothesis aligns with what aged well (IPLD CIDv1, COSE_Sign1, Matrix PDU). The promise-stack framing is a category error -- frame sequences are correct only at the transport/handshake layer. Five derived design invariants: (1) `pcid` unconditional and content-addressed at every layer; (2) payload always bytes (signed before parsed); (3) configurable depth limit; (4) recursion is the right primitive for multi-semantic envelopes; (5) frame-sequences are appropriate only for session handshakes. Five open questions for Steve in section 6 of the research doc: depth budget, `pcid` purity vs version-prefix, capability-reference representation, canonical-serialization rules, onion-routing requirements. (d) Once (a)-(c) are answered, does TE-famar get superseded outright, narrowed to one specific candidate, or absorbed into a new envelope-shape TE?
